5 KV Cache Management

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Agent stack focus for this chapter.

Why can an agent that is generating nothing still exhaust the memory of the machine serving it?

An agent spends much of its life waiting. While a test suite runs or a remote service responds, the model generates nothing, yet the attention state computed from the request’s prefix can stay resident in accelerator memory, holding capacity that another request could put to work. That state is large, it grows with every token the trajectory carries, and its size is fixed by the model’s geometry rather than by any choice the agent makes. A serving engine that reserves room for the longest request it might see strands much of its memory, and one that copies a shared prefix into every branch pays for the same state many times over. At each pause the engine must choose among keeping the state resident, moving it to slower memory, and dropping it to rebuild from the tokens when the agent resumes. Each option trades memory against the delay before the next output token, and the right one depends on how long the wait will last. Capacity is therefore set less by how many contexts fit at once than by how long each one stays, because a context that holds memory through a long tool wait serves no turns while it keeps its place. Managing retained attention state turns the state an agent carries into a priced claim on the machine serving it, sized against the turn rate the service must sustain.

Learning Objectives
  • Calculate retained attention-state memory and resident fit from model geometry, token count, and storage precision
  • Calculate the memory that block allocation and prefix sharing recover from full-cap reservation and duplicated prefixes
  • Distinguish compatible, available prefix reuse from the applicability of the underlying information
  • Calculate the token-weighted prefix hit rate of a radix prefix cache under leaf-first eviction
  • Select a chunked-prefill token budget that bounds the gap between output tokens for ongoing requests
  • Evaluate keep, move, and recompute policies for paused state against memory pressure, resume latency, and lossy approximation risk
  • Analyze how residence time converts a memory-fit bound into a sustainable turn rate
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

5.1 Attention State as a Serving Resource

The connection-pool repair agent (Four lighthouse workloads) has reached a candidate revision, r18. Its targeted reproducer passes, prompting the harness to submit a fully serialized request of 32,768 tokens and launch an integration test suite that runs for 45 seconds. If the serving engine holds the request’s attention state in accelerator memory throughout that entire wait, the device does no work for that workflow. The model generates zero tokens during the test suite, yet its retained state pins memory that another concurrent request could put to work. This is the tool-wait problem named in the turn lifecycle of The six phases of a turn. Preserving that state is a deliberate systems policy, and running an agentic service requires pricing that policy against its alternatives; although this 32,768-token request is shorter than the staged request of the context-engineering examples, every per-request footprint and transfer time below scales linearly with context length.

State becomes a physical resource claim inside the serving system.

Earlier abstractions stop short of that physical commitment. Context Engineering established how the harness selects and refreshes prompt context for each call, and its prefix-layout calculation priced a cached prefix as a discounted line on the bill. Neither abstraction captures the physical footprint of the underlying computation. They do not say how many gigabytes of memory that retained attention state consumes, how many concurrent trajectories an accelerator can hold at once, or what the serving engine should do with that state when an agent pauses to await tool execution. Those mechanical questions decide whether an inference node that comfortably supports a single repair workflow can scale to sustain dozens.

↩︎ Pricing a reused prompt prefix at a discounted cached-input rate is established in Preserving an exact prefix.

Managing retained attention state is not solely an internal serving problem; it spans an operational boundary between two parties. The application caller controls its submitted context, tool execution timing, and any cache-management hints the serving API exposes. The serving operator controls physical device allocation, memory placement, compute scheduling, and retention policies. When the two belong to separate organizations, as with hosted commercial model APIs, the caller cannot manage physical accelerator memory directly. Instead, the application influences retention only through the interfaces and pricing tiers the service exposes. Distinguishing where these responsibilities divide clarifies which performance levers belong to the agent harness and runtime that run the loop around the model, and which belong to the serving engine, the inference software that executes model calls and manages accelerator memory.

The chapter follows the r18 request’s attention state through its life in the serving engine, and each stage raises the question the next one answers. Sizing the state from model geometry shows how few 32,768-token contexts fit in the memory left after the weights (section 5.2). Allocating that state naively strands much of the pool (section 5.3), which fixed-size blocks recover while letting forked candidates share a prefix (section 5.4). Sharing within one request invites reuse across turns, which needs an index over retained prefixes and a rule for what to evict (section 5.5). Whatever the cache misses must be prefilled without stalling other requests’ generation (section 5.6). The test wait itself forces a choice among keeping, moving, and dropping the paused state (section 5.7). Finally, residence time turns a count of resident contexts into a sustainable turn rate (section 5.8).

5.2 The Footprint of Retained Attention State

The r18 request’s 32,768 tokens do not reach the accelerator as raw text. During causal self-attention in a Transformer decoder, each newly generated token attends to earlier tokens through their key and value projections (Vaswani et al. 2017). Recomputing these projections at every decode step would repeat the linear transformations over the entire historical prefix, creating an unsustainable quadratic compute bottleneck. Serving engines avoid this recomputation by retaining the computed key and value tensors in accelerator memory across decode cycles.1 While autoregressive decoding relies on retained attention state, sustaining long-horizon agent execution requires managing its physical capacity, allocation overhead, and multi-tenant residency.

1 Memory-Limited Inference: Caching keys and values trades quadratic recomputation for persistent memory capacity, and that memory quickly becomes the primary serving bottleneck. An analytical study of large-model serving revealed that sharing a single key-value head across all query heads allowed an identical deployment to serve contexts more than an order of magnitude longer, precisely because retained attention state, rather than compute throughput, bounded context length and batch concurrency (Pope et al. 2023).

Pope, Reiner, Sholto Douglas, Aakanksha Chowdhery, Jacob Devlin, James Bradbury, Jonathan Heek, Kefan Xiao, Shivani Agrawal, and Jeff Dean. 2023. “Efficiently Scaling Transformer Inference.” Proceedings of Machine Learning and Systems (MLSys) 5: 606–24.

↩︎ Retained attention state in autoregressive decoding is established in Token-by-token generation.

Definition 5.1: KV cache

KV cache is the per-sequence store of attention keys and values that the model has computed for already processed tokens, retained so that later tokens can attend to them without recomputing their projections.

  1. Significance: Its payload scales linearly with retained tokens; consequently, sequence context length, rather than static model parameter count, determines how many concurrent requests can share the memory pool remaining after loading model weights.
  2. Distinction: It represents intermediate projected state rather than a raw copy or semantic compression of input text. Reconstructing it requires executing the exact token sequence through identical model weights and layer configurations.
  3. Common pitfall: Budgeting only for input prompts. Every newly decoded output token appends key and value states at every layer, so a request reaches its maximum memory footprint at completion rather than during initial prefill.

For a decoder employing causal self-attention with uniform layer geometry, every retained token contributes both a key vector and a value vector across all layers. Let \(\LayerCount\) represent the number of layers, \(\KvHeadCount\) the number of key-value heads per layer, \(\AttentionHeadDimension\) the dimension of each head, and \(\ElementStorageSize\) the storage size in bytes per element. Accounting for both vectors yields the memory footprint per token:

\[\KvCacheBytesPerToken = 2 \cdot \LayerCount \cdot \KvHeadCount \cdot \AttentionHeadDimension \cdot \ElementStorageSize \tag{1}\]

A sequence retaining attention state across \(\SequenceLength\) tokens requires an aggregate cache payload of \(\KvCacheMemory = \SequenceLength \cdot \KvCacheBytesPerToken\). Here \(\SequenceLength\) counts only the tokens whose keys and values have already been evaluated; each newly sampled token increments \(\SequenceLength\) once the model completes its forward pass.

The key-value head count \(\KvHeadCount\) in equation 1 represents the number of stored attention heads, which often falls below the query head count. Multi-query attention pushed this reduction to its structural extreme by sharing a single key-value head across all query heads2 (Shazeer 2019). Grouped-query attention strikes an intermediate balance: several query heads share one key-value head, dividing the stored key-value head count by the group size relative to a standard multi-head design (Ainslie et al. 2023), a scaling quantified across attention designs in Attention State by Attention Design. Because this head grouping is established during pretraining, a serving allocator cannot dynamically drop heads from an existing checkpoint without changing the model’s computations.

2 Multi-Query Attention: Motivated primarily by memory bandwidth rather than storage capacity (incremental decoding reloads stored key and value tensors at each generation step), sharing key-value tensors across query heads drastically reduces memory bus traffic alongside physical storage with minimal quality loss (Shazeer 2019). Grouped-query attention subsequently recovered nearly all multi-head attention quality while preserving the bulk of these memory savings (Ainslie et al. 2023).

Shazeer, Noam. 2019. “Fast Transformer Decoding: One Write-Head Is All You Need.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1911.02150.
Ainslie, Joshua, James Lee-Thorp, Michiel de Jong, Yury Zemlyanskiy, Federico Lebr’on, and Sumit Sanghai. 2023. “GQA: Training Generalized Multi-Query Transformer Models from Multi-Head Checkpoints.” Proceedings of the 2023 Conference on Empirical Methods in Natural Language Processing (EMNLP).

While equation 1 accounts for the raw tensor payload under dense causal attention, physical memory consumption in production serving engines exceeds this theoretical bound. Allocation block granularities, memory alignment, page table metadata, and quantization scales all add runtime overhead. Non-uniform architectures, such as sliding-window attention or compressed latent representations, require their own capacity models.

None of the r18 context’s internal structure survives into equation 1. In figure 1, the prompt zones follow the change-rate order of context staging: a shared invariant prefix of system instructions, tool schemas, and slow-changing workspace description, followed by the growing trajectory state of turn history and the active turn’s latest observation. Once processed, every zone becomes identical-format key and value tensors at every layer, so the entire 32,768-token context occupies 10.7 GB regardless of what its zones contain.

To translate that per-context payload into node capacity, the worked estimate below uses a representative configuration: the Llama 3 70B model geometry, with 70.6B parameters, and a DGX H100 node containing 8 accelerators. While specific hardware platforms and checkpoint dimensions vary, the underlying capacity arithmetic remains unchanged. Unlike the single-device, 8-bit illustration in Estimating per-call hardware work, both weights and KV cache tensors use 16-bit storage here, with the model weights sharded across the node’s accelerators by tensor parallelism. The aggregate high-bandwidth memory remaining after provisioning model weights and a runtime reserve constitutes the KV pool. The number of active sequences whose KV payloads can reside simultaneously within this pool defines the resident-fit bound. This aggregate derivation assumes perfectly balanced shards without redundant weight replication; in production, memory limits must be satisfied on every individual accelerator device.

Napkin Math 5.1: Sizing the attention state of an agent trajectory
Problem: Consider a server node of 8 accelerators, each with 80 GB (vendor-rated in binary gigabytes, about 85.9 GB in the decimal units used throughout this chapter), for 687.2 GB of aggregate accelerator memory. Model weights occupy 141.2 GB, and the runtime reserves 20 GB for dynamic activations and scratch buffers. Under full causal attention without prefix sharing, how many independent sequences can simultaneously reside in the remaining memory across different context lengths?

Math: Evaluating equation 1 for this architecture yields \(\KvCacheBytesPerToken = 2 \times 80 \times 8 \times 128 \times 2 = 327,680\;\text{bytes/token}\). For an agent trajectory retaining 32,768 tokens, the footprint becomes \(\KvCacheMemory = 32,768 \times 327,680\;\text{bytes} \approx 10.7\;\text{GB}\).

Subtracting model parameters and runtime buffers isolates the usable capacity: \(687.2 - 141.2 - 20 \approx 526.0\;\text{GB}\). Dividing this available pool by the footprint per sequence and taking the floor determines the resident-fit bound. Using exact floating-point values prior to display rounding yields \(\left\lfloor 525.994767360 / 10.737418240 \right\rfloor = 48\). Table 1 evaluates this bound across three operational context regimes.

Systems insight: Resident capacity scales inversely with retained context length. Because model weights occupy a fixed memory slice regardless of context, expanding sequence length directly cannibalizes the cache pool. Each fourfold increase in sequence length (from 8,192 to 32,768, and onward to 131,072 tokens) reduces the resident sequence capacity by roughly 75 percent. Retained context length is the primary control lever governing serving capacity, and the agent runtime directly governs it. Maintaining an active working set rather than accumulating unbounded historical observations (principle 4) keeps requests operating in the high-density rows of table 1. These figures are raw tensor payloads; physical memory allocators, page fragmentation, and safety headroom further reduce actual capacity, while translating instantaneous resident capacity into sustainable request throughput requires analyzing sequence residence times (section 5.8.2).

Because model weights incur a one-time memory cost amortized across all concurrent requests, every independent sequence adds its private attention state to the pool unless the serving engine caches and reuses common prefixes across turns. Within each sequence, model architecture and numerical precision set the per-token payload before memory allocation even begins. An architecture trained with fewer key-value heads or compressed latent representations3 reduces the state generated per token. Quantizing keys and values to 8-bit or 4-bit representations halves or quarters the raw element bytes \(\ElementStorageSize\). It does so at the cost of dequantization scale metadata and potential numerical degradation, which engineers must validate against target task benchmarks. Regardless of compression, every retained byte remains allocated physical memory tied to an active execution lifecycle. How much the architecture moves that payload is easiest to see across published geometries. Figure 2 evaluates equation 1, and its latent-attention counterpart, for six registered models: one with full multi-head attention, four with grouped-query attention, and one with multi-head latent attention.

3 Latent KV Compression: Multi-head latent attention compresses keys and values into a single low-rank latent vector per token and dynamically reconstructs per-head tensors during attention, exchanging additional projection arithmetic for a substantially smaller memory footprint (DeepSeek-AI et al. 2024). Although this technique modifies the calculation of \(\KvCacheBytesPerToken\) in equation 1, it preserves the same system dynamics: latent cache tensors must still be paged, allocated, retained across idle periods, and evicted under memory pressure.

DeepSeek-AI, Aixin Liu, Bei Feng, Bin Wang, Bingxuan Wang, et al. 2024. “DeepSeek-V2: A Strong, Economical, and Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Language Model.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2405.04434, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2405.04434.

The spread in figure 2 is fixed at pretraining; the serving engine can work within it but cannot change it. Full multi-head attention in the GPT-3 (175B) geometry retains 4,718.6 KB per token, 67× the 70.3 KB of the latent-attention DeepSeek-V3; even the grouped-query Llama-3.1-70B of the worked estimate retains 4.7× as much. At the 131,072 tokens of the long regime, that is 42.9 GB against 9.2 GB for one session. The choice of model therefore fixes the slope of every capacity line in this chapter, and the runtime’s levers (retained tokens, sharing, precision, and eviction) act only along that slope. Whatever the slope, each retained byte stays allocated for as long as its sequence holds the prefix, which is where the lifecycle decisions begin.

Model geometry and numerical precision fix the raw tensor payload per token, setting an idealized ceiling on how many sequences fit in the KV pool at once. Reaching that ceiling in a live service, however, requires translating theoretical tensor footprints into physical memory allocations for sequences whose final length is unknown when they arrive. If the allocator statically pre-allocates contiguous buffers for each request’s maximum potential context, unused reservation slack exhausts the memory pool long before actual tensor payloads fill device capacity.

5.3 Memory Fragmentation

When the repair agent runs an integration test and receives a compiler traceback or test log, its next generation step needs KV cache space for both its accumulated history and the newly ingested output. Yet neither the harness nor the serving engine knows in advance how many tokens the model will generate or how many tool turns will follow. A static footprint calculation sizes the tokens processed so far. The memory allocator governs what happens as sequences grow: how much spare buffer to reserve at admission, whether an expanding sequence must be copied to a larger region in memory, and how quickly freed capacity returns to the shared pool.

5.3.1 Reservation and growth

Provisioning a contiguous memory buffer for the full configured token cap (\(\ContextWindow\)) the moment a request arrives guarantees that a growing sequence never stalls for reallocation or runs out of memory mid-generation. Yet it locks every unpopulated slot for the entire life of the request, so no concurrent sequence can use that capacity. This upfront policy is static reservation: because the maximum buffer is secured at admission, the sequence expands in place within its pre-allocated envelope. The reservation covers only KV cache state; activation tensors and temporary runtime scratchpads draw from separate memory pools.

In the 526.0 GB KV pool modeled above, an admission cap of 32,768 tokens requires 10.74 GB for each reservation. Dividing the pool by this single-request footprint bounds concurrency to at most 48 resident reservations, assuming balanced tensor-parallel sharding across accelerators and no auxiliary overhead beyond the baseline runtime reserve. The count matches the 32,768-token row of table 1 because a full-cap reservation is exactly that context’s footprint, whether or not the tokens ever arrive.

Now consider the resulting capacity utilization for a mixed request population, mostly short turns with occasional long trajectories like r18, all admitted under the same cap. If those requests finish at a mean length of 2,048 tokens, each reservation is only 6.25 percent populated even upon completion, holding 2,048 valid token states out of 32,768 allocated slots. During earlier decoding steps, utilization is lower still. Empirical evaluations confirm that this structural waste dominates naive serving systems: when Kwon et al. (2023) profiled early systems, actual token state accounted for only 20.4 to 38.2 percent of allocated KV memory, with the remainder lost to reservation slack and fragmentation.

A second strategy grows a contiguous allocation incrementally as tokens arrive. When adjacent capacity is unavailable in the allocator’s address space, the memory manager cannot simply extend the buffer in place. Instead, it must allocate a larger buffer elsewhere, copy all existing key and value entries from the old buffer to the new buffer, and then release the original space. This approach incurs two severe penalties: the allocator must hold both buffers simultaneously during the transfer, causing transient memory spikes, and the synchronous copy consumes substantial memory bandwidth. Contiguity here refers to an unbroken span in the framework allocator’s virtual address space, even though the accelerator already translates virtual addresses to physical memory in hardware. The restriction comes from how standard attention kernels index memory rather than from the memory hardware itself. Because a conventional attention kernel locates each token’s keys and values by adding a fixed stride to a single base pointer, the allocator must keep a sequence’s entire KV tensor in one unbroken buffer and copy it whenever growth runs into an adjacent allocation. Hardware page tables alone do not remove that bottleneck for the serving engine, because the policies that govern KV reuse operate on token blocks in user space: how many tokens a unit of allocation holds, how many candidate sequences reference each unit, and when a shared unit must be copied before a write. Putting that indirection in a software block table that the attention kernel reads directly eliminates contiguous-buffer reallocation while keeping block sizing and sharing under the serving engine’s control (section 5.4).

5.3.2 Two kinds of unused capacity

Static reservation and incremental contiguous growth each strand capacity in a different place. Static reservation locks thousands of unpopulated slots inside active allocations, whereas variable-size contiguous buffers leave free capacity split across disconnected gaps too small to satisfy a new contiguous request even when aggregate free memory is ample. Unused slots locked inside an active reservation constitute internal fragmentation; free capacity split across disconnected extents too small to satisfy a contiguous request constitutes external fragmentation. This classic distinction originates in early storage allocation research for multiprogrammed systems4 (Randell 1969). Table 2 separates them because each condition demands a distinct systems remedy: eliminating reservation slack cures the first, whereas changing the allocation granularity cures the second.

4 Internal and External Fragmentation: The analysis of storage allocation that named the two losses also measured their trade: rounding requests up to fewer standard sizes cost more storage in internal fragmentation than it recovered in external fragmentation (Randell 1969). Paged KV allocation makes the same trade on purpose, accepting at most one partial block per sequence in exchange for a pool in which any free block fits any request.

Randell, Brian. 1969. “A Note on Storage Fragmentation and Program Segmentation.” Communications of the ACM 12 (7): 365–69, 372. https://doi.org/10.1145/363156.363158.

Suppose an allocator has three free extents of 256 token slots each, separated by live allocations, when a new sequence arrives requesting 512 contiguous slots. Even though the allocator holds 768 free slots in aggregate (more than enough capacity to satisfy the request), the allocation fails because no single extent can hold 512 contiguous slots. The failure stems entirely from the contiguity requirement rather than an absolute shortage of memory.

In an agentic repair workload, this memory pressure becomes acute because a paused trajectory retains its allocation under naive retention policies while waiting for tool execution, even as concurrent requests demand memory to grow. A robust serving system therefore requires two complementary architectural mechanisms: first, a space-efficient allocation unit, which fixed-size blocks provide, and second, an explicit lifecycle and eviction policy, which section 5.7 develops.

5.4 Paged KV Allocation

When an integration-test log extends an agent’s repair context, the serving engine need not relocate the existing KV tensor to find a larger contiguous buffer. Instead, it allocates additional fixed-size physical blocks and appends their identifiers to the request’s block table. The attention kernel dereferences that table when reading earlier token positions. PagedAttention demonstrated this combination of dynamic block allocation and noncontiguous attention over KV storage (Kwon et al. 2023). Its lineage traces directly to paged virtual memory. The Manchester Atlas one-level store introduced paging in 1962 to decouple a program’s contiguous virtual address space from scattered physical page frames (Kilburn et al. 1962). The block table applies this same indirection to token positions, allowing sequences to grow dynamically without requiring contiguous physical allocations.

Definition 5.2: Paged KV block table

Paged KV block table is a per-sequence software mapping from logical token blocks to the physical KV blocks that store their initialized key and value entries.

  1. Significance: A sequence can grow by acquiring another block without relocating its existing blocks.
  2. Distinction: The table maps token positions for the attention kernel; it is not the accelerator’s hardware page table.
  3. Common pitfall: A mapped block has allocated capacity, but only its initialized token entries may be read as valid KV state.

5.4.1 Token-to-block mapping

Once physical blocks sit scattered across accelerator memory, the attention kernel can no longer locate a token’s keys and values by adding a fixed stride to a single base pointer. It must resolve each token position through the sequence’s block table. Consider an illustrative block size of four tokens, chosen to make the mapping concrete rather than to recommend a production configuration. A ten-token prefix occupies logical blocks 0, 1, and 2. Its block table contains [3, 7, 9]: token positions 0–3 reside in physical block 3, positions 4–7 in block 7, and positions 8–9 in the first two slots of block 9. Physical block IDs are pool indices managed by the allocator, not raw memory addresses or token sequence numbers.

For any token position, the attention kernel determines its logical block index via integer division by the block size, consults the block table to locate the physical block, and calculates the token’s offset within that block using the modulo remainder. For token position 9 with a four-token block size, the kernel calculates logical block \(\lfloor 9 / 4 \rfloor = 2\), looks up physical block 9, and determines offset \(9 \bmod 4 = 1\) (the second slot). Computing the final byte offset in device memory incorporates the model layer, KV head index, and tensor stride conventions. This software lookup preserves logical sequence ordering even when physical blocks are scattered across high-bandwidth memory among other requests’ blocks, as panel (a) of figure 3 traces for position 9.

Because every block in the allocator’s pool is interchangeable and of identical size, any free block satisfies an allocation request. Paging thereby eliminates external memory fragmentation at block granularity. The remaining memory overhead consists of block-table metadata, temporary scratchpad buffers, and unallocated slots in each sequence’s final block.

5.4.2 Block size and tail waste

In the ten-token prefix traced in figure 3, physical block 9 holds positions 8 and 9 in its first two slots while its remaining two slots sit empty. Because sequence lengths rarely align with block boundaries, the final block of a sequence typically contains such uninitialized slots. This internal fragmentation constitutes the tail waste \(\KvBlockTailWaste\). For a block holding \(\KvBlockSize\) tokens, tail waste is bounded by \(0 \leq \KvBlockTailWaste \leq \KvBlockSize-1\), with an expected value of \((\KvBlockSize-1)/2\) when sequence lengths yield uniformly distributed remainders modulo \(\KvBlockSize\). A sequence whose length is an exact multiple of \(\KvBlockSize\) incurs zero tail waste.

Block size trades allocation granularity against indexing overhead. Smaller blocks (for example, 16 tokens) reduce worst-case tail waste and permit fine-grained prefix sharing and copy-on-write duplication, though they inflate block-table metadata and increase indexing overhead for the attention kernel. Larger blocks (for example, 64 tokens) amortize bookkeeping costs and align more effectively with memory bus transactions and tensor core tile dimensions on modern accelerators, at the cost of greater tail waste and copy overhead during branch forks. Selecting an appropriate block size therefore requires balancing sequence length distributions, tree-search branching patterns, and hardware memory access characteristics.

Napkin Math 5.2: Converting block size into KV storage
Problem: Given the Llama 3 70B geometry and a 16-bit KV representation, what physical storage granularity and maximum tail slack result from two candidate block sizes?

Math: Each token requires 327680 bytes across all model layers and attention heads. Multiplying that unit footprint by 16 tokens yields 5.24 MB per block. Multiplying by 64 tokens yields 20.97 MB. These values are node-wide footprints summed across all model shards, not the local allocation on a single accelerator.

The maximum unused tail contains one fewer slot than a full block. A 16-token block can therefore leave at most 15 slots (4.92 MB) unused, while a 64-token block can leave at most 63 slots (20.64 MB) unused.

For a 32,768-token agent context, even the larger block’s worst-case tail waste represents only 0.2 percent of the sequence’s total memory footprint.

Systems insight: Because tail slack is bounded by at most one block per unshared sequence, its relative overhead diminishes as sequence length increases. At multi-thousand-token agent context lengths, tail waste ceases to be the governing constraint. Engineers should select block sizes primarily to optimize attention kernel throughput and sharing granularity, revisiting tail waste only when serving workloads dominated by short sequences.

The final-length snapshot estimate is about 16× the full-cap reservation count.

Translating these worst-case bounds into expected memory overhead requires evaluating the workload’s sequence length distribution. Consider final sequence lengths modeled as a capped exponential distribution with a mean of 2,048 tokens, bounded by the 32,768-token ceiling established for static reservation. Under this distribution, 16-token blocks leave 0.4 percent of allocated KV memory unused, whereas 64-token blocks leave 1.5 percent.

Dividing the same pool, which accommodated only 48 static worst-case reservations, by the expected paged footprint gives a planning estimate of approximately 780 final-length request snapshots, a 16× increase over full-cap reservation. This capacity multiplier represents the core payoff of paging: memory allocation expands incrementally as tokens are generated, rather than reserving buffer space for an upper bound that most requests never reach. Because the gain is close to the ratio of the cap to the mean final length (32,768 to 2,048), it measures how far the population falls short of the cap. This snapshot estimate describes a hypothetical population sampled at completion; the requests resident in a running service have a length distribution that also depends on growth and residence time, and actual admission must account for current unique blocks and promised generation growth. Moreover, a request like r18 that actually grows to the cap gains no final-length capacity from paging. What paging still buys it is growth without relocation, memory claimed only as tokens arrive rather than at admission, and block-level sharing with forked candidates.

5.4.3 Copy-on-write prefix sharing

When a repair agent samples parallel candidate patches from the same prompt and tool-execution prefix to verify them empirically (principle 3), giving each branch its own contiguous KV buffer duplicates gigabytes of identical prefix state across every candidate. The breadth allocation of Parallel sampling along the breadth axis already priced this difference: with the prompt stored once, peak KV demand grows as the shared prefix plus each candidate’s private tokens, whereas without sharing every candidate carries its own copy of the prompt. Paged allocation delivers the shared form by pointing every candidate’s block table to the same physical blocks across the common prefix whenever the model architecture and runtime configuration allow block sharing.

The difference is large at agent context scales. Under contiguous allocation, giving each of four candidate branches its own copy of an 8,192-token prefix requires writing 10.7 GB of KV state for the Llama 3 70B geometry, counting the four new copies and not the parent’s original. That is the footprint of one full 32,768-token context, since four copies of 8,192 tokens hold that many token positions. Under paged allocation, duplicating the block tables avoids copying any tensor payload. The engine updates software metadata and increments reference counters; cloning a block table requires work proportional to the number of block entries rather than the roughly ten gigabytes of underlying KV data.

A shared sequence tail requires special handling when branches diverge. If the final block of a shared prefix is only partially filled, two concurrent candidates cannot independently append new tokens into its remaining empty slots without overwriting each other. In the ten-token example, both candidates initially share the block table [3, 7, 9]. Physical block 9 contains only two valid tokens (positions 8 and 9 in slots 0 and 1). Before candidate B writes token position 10, the engine allocates a fresh physical block 14, copies the two initialized entries from block 9 into block 14, and updates candidate B’s final block-table entry to point to block 14. Candidate B then writes token 10 into slot 2 of its private block 14. This copy-on-write5 operation leaves candidate A’s state unchanged, as panel (b) of figure 3 draws. Table 3 records the ownership transition, assuming these two candidates are the sole owners of the prefix.

5 Copy-on-Write: The TENEX time-sharing system of the early 1970s gave each page a status bit that let processes share large parts of an address space and obtain private copies of only the pages they changed (Bobrow et al. 1972). Forked candidates reuse the idea at block granularity, which is why a fan-out of sampled repairs costs private blocks in proportion to what each candidate writes, not to the prefix they share.

Bobrow, Daniel G., Jerry D. Burchfiel, Daniel L. Murphy, and Raymond S. Tomlinson. 1972. “TENEX, a Paged Time Sharing System for the PDP-10.” Communications of the ACM 15 (3): 135–43. https://doi.org/10.1145/361268.361271.

Copy-on-write is necessary only when mutating a shared, partially filled block. When a candidate branches at an exact block boundary (for instance, after a twelve-token prefix using four-token blocks), token position 12 initiates a new logical block (\(\lfloor 12 / 4 \rfloor = 3\)). The generating candidate allocates an unshared block from the free pool and appends its ID to its block table, requiring no data copying. Similarly, if a partial tail block is already privately owned (reference count of 1), the candidate appends subsequent tokens directly into the remaining slots without allocation or copying. The engine selects the appropriate operation based on two state variables: the occupancy of the target block and its current reference count.

When an agent discards an unpromising candidate trajectory, the engine decrements the reference counts of its constituent physical blocks. A block returns to the free pool when its reference count reaches zero and all outstanding device operations (tracked via asynchronous completion events) have finished. A dormant prefix-cache entry (section 5.5) may also retain physical blocks even after an active request completes, preserving them across subsequent agent turns. Total physical memory consumption is therefore governed by the number of unique physical blocks resident on the accelerator, not the aggregate logical token count across all active candidates.

Checkpoint 5.1: Reasoning about KV allocation

The allocation policy must preserve each sequence’s state while managing capacity shared by many requests.

Paged allocation decouples logical sequence expansion from physical memory layout, eliminating external fragmentation while enabling zero-copy prefix sharing across concurrent trajectories. Sharing state across independent requests or across subsequent turns of an agent session requires a complementary mechanism, prefix caching, in which the serving engine identifies matching historical prefixes across disparate requests and manages cache retention under memory pressure.

5.5 Prefix Caching Across Turns

In the connection-pool repair scenario, the agent invocation that processes an integration-test result often begins with the identical system prompt, tool definitions, and historical observations present in the preceding turn. If the serving engine retained the corresponding key-value (KV) activations from that earlier call, it can attach those existing tensors directly to the new request, prefilling only the newly arrived tokens. On the bill, this saving appears as a discounted cached-input rate; in system implementation, it translates into a concrete serving architecture governed by an index, an explicit ownership contract, and an eviction policy.

Definition 5.3: Prefix cache

Prefix cache is the serving system’s index of retained KV state, keyed by exact token prefix and computation configuration, allowing subsequent requests sharing that initial sequence to attach existing state instead of recomputing it during prefill.

  1. Significance: It converts repeated prefill work into an index lookup across request boundaries. The computational savings grow with the length of the matched prefix and recur on every turn that preserves that prefix, making stable prompt layouts essential across long trajectories.
  2. Distinction: Block sharing reuses state among candidates that the engine forks from one request and therefore knows to be related. A prefix cache must find reusable state, by content, for requests that arrive independently over time, and it can offer only what memory pressure and eviction have left resident.
  3. Common pitfall: Assuming a cache hit guarantees semantic freshness. Matching cached tokens verifies identical historical inputs and execution settings, but it does not check whether the real-world state described by those tokens has changed.

5.5.1 Prefix compatibility

Attaching cached KV blocks from an earlier turn corrupts generation if a single upstream token, adapter weight, or positional encoding differs between the two calls. The reason is that, under causal full attention, every token’s keys and values depend on the exact token prefix and execution configuration that produced them (principle 5). Matching token identifiers from the first position of the sequence is only the first prerequisite. The cached KV tensors remain mathematically valid only when generated under identical execution parameters: the same base model weights, active low-rank adaptation (LoRA) adapters (lightweight fine-tuned weight adjustments), positional encodings, and attention masks. For multimodal requests containing images, audio, or structured embeddings, matching the synthetic placeholder tokens in the text prompt is insufficient; the underlying tensor inputs and projection parameters must match exactly. A production serving engine must encode these operational dependencies directly into its cache keys or validate them before binding cached blocks to a request.

Even when a cached prefix is mathematically compatible, the engine still has to verify that its blocks are physically resident and permitted to the caller. Computationally compatible tensors may have already been evicted from accelerator high-bandwidth memory (HBM), or they may reside in host memory or on solid-state drives, requiring an explicit page-in transfer before the model can consume them. Conversely, tensors residing directly in local accelerator memory may belong to a different tenant or security domain, making them ineligible for sharing under isolation rules. The reusable prefix length is the longest contiguous token sequence that simultaneously satisfies compatibility, physical residency, and access permissions. That sequence is frequently shorter than the longest match found in the prefix tree. Furthermore, newly generated tokens qualify for subsequent prefix matching only after their corresponding KV blocks have been fully materialized in memory and committed to the index.

These structural requirements operate independently of semantic validity. Updating a target repository from commit r17 to r18 does not alter or invalidate the KV activations previously computed for an older prompt describing r17. Those tensors remain numerically identical and computationally compatible with the stale prompt, even though the context no longer reflects the current state of the code. The harness’s context-selection policies govern which evidence to provide to the model. When the agent harness updates or replaces outdated logs in the serialized prompt, the prefix matcher detects the divergence, immediately terminates the match at the first altered token, and triggers a fresh prefill for the updated text. The prompt layout, rather than the cache layer, enforces freshness.

↩︎ How the harness withdraws a stale observation, such as the r17 test failure, while keeping it as history is established in Context Invalidation.

5.5.2 Radix prefix lookup

Matching a new request against thousands of retained multi-thousand-token agent prompts token by token would build a deep, pointer-heavy index. A radix prefix cache avoids this by compressing chains of single-child token transitions into variable-length sequence edges in a radix tree (Zheng et al. 2024).6 To resolve a cache match, the scheduler walks the tree from the root, matching prompt tokens along successive edges until reaching a divergence point. This traversal simultaneously identifies the longest common prefix and the physical KV blocks storing its activations. Once a match is identified, the engine must acquire explicit ownership of those memory blocks before the background eviction thread can reclaim them. Lookup overhead scales with the prefix length examined, remaining on the order of microseconds on host CPUs. Alternatively, systems can implement an equivalent reuse contract using chained hash tables, where each fixed-size block is keyed by a cryptographic hash of its token content concatenated with the hash of its parent prefix.

6 Compressed Prefix Tree: The trie stored one symbol per edge, so a shared prefix was found by walking from the root (Fredkin 1960). PATRICIA collapsed chains of single-child nodes into one edge, so index size grows with the number of stored keys rather than their length (Morrison 1968). The same compression matters for KV reuse, because agent prefixes run to thousands of tokens and a one-token-per-edge index would spend a node on each of them.

Fredkin, Edward. 1960. “Trie Memory.” Communications of the ACM 3 (9): 490–99. https://doi.org/10.1145/367390.367400.
Morrison, Donald R. 1968. “PATRICIA—Practical Algorithm To Retrieve Information Coded in Alphanumeric.” Journal of the ACM 15 (4): 514–34. https://doi.org/10.1145/321479.321481.

The tree’s shape follows from the causal dependence that context staging already turned into a layout rule. Every position’s keys and values depend on the full history before it, so a node’s KV state is valid only beneath its exact ancestors. An identical passage that follows a modified token therefore lives on a different branch with no reusable state. The layout rule is the harness’s half of the same constraint. Staging volatile components (dynamic counters, tool outputs, scratchpad notes) toward the end of the prompt keeps the long invariant prefix on one path from the root, and compaction that rewrites only trailing turns leaves that path intact.

↩︎ Stable-prefix layout rules for prompt staging are established in Staging the Next Invocation.

Physical memory allocation introduces a practical policy choice at block boundaries without altering the underlying definition of compatibility. Suppose a later request matches the ten-token prefix whose blocks [3, 7, 9] were traced earlier, with block 9 holding two initialized entries. A serving engine that supports partial-tail reuse preserves all ten tokens and resolves the new turn’s divergent append with the same copy-on-write step recorded in table 3. An engine that caches only complete blocks reuses eight tokens and prefills the remaining two. Both designs are valid. The partial-tail strategy minimizes redundant prefill at the expense of copy-on-write buffer management, while the full-block strategy simplifies allocation by trading a modest amount of recomputation for uniform block lifecycles.

5.5.3 Retention and eviction under pressure

Keeping finished requests’ KV blocks around as dormant cache entries allows subsequent turns to reuse them. Those dormant blocks, however, eventually fill the KV pool and force the allocator to evict old entries without racing active kernels or breaking shared tree paths. When an inference request finishes, the serving engine releases the request’s execution handles while the cache manager retains its own structural references to the blocks. The system’s retention policy then determines whether the next turn finds that KV state still resident in accelerator memory, pages it back from host RAM, or recomputes it from tokens.

Reusing and reclaiming memory blocks requires strict ownership tracking across concurrent threads and hardware streams. When a request matches a cached prefix, the memory manager increments reference counts on the corresponding blocks before dispatching execution kernels. Conversely, before reclaiming a physical block under memory pressure, the allocator must sever the cache’s indexing reference, confirm that active execution references have dropped to zero, and synchronize with outstanding device streams that might still be reading or writing those memory addresses. The allocator must also synchronize index modifications and reference changes so that a concurrent lookup cannot bind to a block while background eviction is tearing it down.

In tree-structured prefix caches, leaf-first eviction maintains structural consistency by preserving contiguous paths from the root and evicting unused leaf nodes first. Because internal nodes store common prefix state shared across multiple conversation branches, evicting an ancestor would invalidate all of its downstream descendants. An internal node becomes eligible for reclamation only after all of its descendant children have been evicted. Within the set of eligible leaves, systems frequently adopt a least-recently-used (LRU) policy (Zheng et al. 2024), operating on the premise that recent access predicts near-term reuse (Belady 1966).7 Reclamation therefore cascades upward. An inactive parent cannot be reclaimed while its child remains cached, but once the child leaf is evicted, the parent itself becomes an eligible leaf and can be freed if memory pressure persists (figure 4).

7 Replacement with Foresight: The classic study of page replacement showed that the optimal policy evicts the page whose next use lies furthest in the future, a rule no online system can apply because it needs the future reference string (Belady 1966). Recency-based policies approximate it. An agent harness sometimes holds part of that future, such as a tool launch that will idle a prefix for minutes, which is why a retention hint from the caller can beat recency alone.

Belady, Laszlo A. 1966. “A Study of Replacement Algorithms for a Virtual-Storage Computer.” IBM Systems Journal 5 (2): 78–101. https://doi.org/10.1147/sj.52.0078.

Leaf-first eviction keeps residency prefix-closed, meaning that every resident node’s ancestors are also resident, and in full-attention models that property is an operational necessity. While freeing an upstream prefix block leaves the numeric values of a retained downstream suffix physically untouched, causal attention requires the entire preceding context to evaluate subsequent tokens. An orphaned suffix block is useless on the accelerator until its missing ancestral prefix is either reloaded from auxiliary storage or recomputed through a new prefill pass.

Retention policies therefore balance occupied accelerator capacity against the computational cost a future hit would eliminate. Recency alone provides an imperfect signal: an agent may generate a speculative branch that is never visited again, whereas a system prompt shared by hundreds of concurrent agent workers remains valuable despite sporadic gaps between calls. More resilient eviction schemes score cached nodes by balancing their expected reuse probability and recomputation cost against their physical memory footprint, adapting the retention weights to match dynamic workload demands.

5.5.4 Prefix hit rate and the reuse contract

Counting raw matching tokens in a prompt overstates real-world system benefits whenever matching blocks have been evicted, tenant isolation policies prohibit sharing, or memory allocators round matches down to physical block boundaries. Systems engineers evaluate caching efficacy by measuring the tokens actually served from reusable KV state without recomputation. Across a specified workload window, the token-weighted prefix hit rate is defined as

\[\PrefixHitRate = \frac{\sum_{i} \ReusedPrefixLength^{(\WorkloadRequestIndex)}}{\sum_{i} \PromptLength^{(\WorkloadRequestIndex)}} \tag{2}\]

where \(\PromptLength^{(\WorkloadRequestIndex)}\) represents the prompt token count of request \(\WorkloadRequestIndex\), and \(\ReusedPrefixLength^{(\WorkloadRequestIndex)}\) counts the prompt tokens whose compatible KV activations were attached without recomputation after enforcing availability, security permissions, and block-alignment constraints. In the earlier four-token block example, a request matching ten tokens under a full-block-only caching policy yields \(\ReusedPrefixLength = 8\), leaving two tokens to be prefilled. A production telemetry pipeline should also distinguish warm hits (state already resident in accelerator HBM) from cold hits (state that must be restored across PCIe from host memory).

A 4,096-token prefix of invariant system instructions and tool definitions, smaller than the shared zone of the r18 context, already shows the scale of the prefill work a hit avoids. Retaining this sequence in memory occupies 1.34 GB of KV activations distributed across the server node. Reconstructing that state from scratch requires roughly 578 trillion operations for linear projection layers, plus approximately 22 trillion operations for causal self-attention (equation 4). If the node sustains 45 percent of its peak 16-bit floating-point throughput (about 3,560 trillion operations per second), rebuilding that prefix incurs 169 ms of prefill latency on every cache miss. In contrast, a turn that scores a cache hit and appends only a 32-token suffix bypasses the prefix prefill entirely, requiring only a single memory-bandwidth-bound pass over the model weights. Because a 32-token suffix provides too little parallel arithmetic to saturate the accelerator’s matrix units, execution time is bounded by the weight-read floor of roughly 5 ms. In general, a weight-only forward pass over \(\ForwardPassTokenCount\) token positions takes \(\WeightOnlyStepTime(\ForwardPassTokenCount) = \max\left(\PerDeviceWeightReadTime,\; 2\Parameters \ForwardPassTokenCount/\AttainableComputeRate\right)\), the larger of the time \(\PerDeviceWeightReadTime\) to read the weights once from accelerator memory and the time to perform \(2\Parameters \ForwardPassTokenCount\) weight operations at the sustained rate \(\AttainableComputeRate\). These are the two per-token floors of equation 2.7 applied to a whole pass (equation 5 derives the bound and its assumptions, while the full prefix rebuild adds the quadratic attention term). The hit thus replaces 169 ms of prefix prefill with a pass of about 5 ms, and a trajectory that keeps its invariant prefix stable collects that saving on every turn, in both latency and aggregate prefill work.

Although equation 2 measures the computational work avoided during prefill, end-to-end request latency also includes novel suffix prefill, autoregressive token generation, batch scheduling delays, and memory transfers. Once a hit eliminates prefix compute, any of these can dominate total response time. When monitoring production clusters, a declining prefix hit rate warrants investigating both sides of the client-service interface. On the client side, minor modifications in prompt serialization (such as altered whitespace, injected timestamps, or reordered JSON schema keys) prematurely truncate prefix matches. On the server side, heightened memory pressure, poor cross-node routing, or mismatched runtime configurations can render compatible state unavailable or physically inaccessible.

When using hosted model APIs, application developers require an explicit reuse contract rather than relying on unverified assumptions about the provider’s internal memory allocator. This contract must clearly define eligible token inputs, compatible execution configurations, tenant isolation boundaries, and supported retention lifetimes. In agentic workflows, cache expiration tightly couples this reuse contract to tool execution latency. If an external tool call blocks longer than the provider’s idle eviction threshold, the subsequent turn discovers that its cached prefix has been purged and incurs a full prefill penalty. This is the remote equivalent of the drop-and-recompute policy described in section 5.7. An agent harness aware of these eviction horizons can anticipate cache misses during long tool waits rather than absorbing unexpected latency spikes. The cached-token metric returned in API usage records is the client’s primary telemetry signal, though interpreting it requires understanding its precise operational scope.

Systems Perspective 5.1: Reading a cached-token count
A usage record that reports cached input tokens indicates how many prompt tokens the provider served from reusable KV state, which is the numerator of equation 2 for that specific request. However, this metric remains silent on where those tensors resided (accelerator HBM vs. host DRAM), how the provider indexed them, or how long they will remain unevicted before the next invocation.

Its diagnostic value emerges when tracked across successive turns of an agent trajectory. Dividing the cached-token count by the total prompt tokens yields the caller’s observed prefix hit rate. A count that abruptly plunges to zero following an extended tool execution is consistent with cache expiration under idle eviction, but memory pressure, routing changes, and incompatible execution settings can produce the same observation. A count that stabilizes below the expected value may indicate an early prompt serialization divergence. Comparing submitted token prefixes and checking request routing and execution settings helps distinguish that client-side cause from unavailable or ineligible cached state. A confirmed layout divergence is an issue the client application can resolve directly in its prompt assembly pipeline.

An agent application must maintain functional correctness regardless of whether a request hits or misses the cache; the serialized context, not the cache, dictates the model’s true prompt input. A cache hit eliminates redundant arithmetic. When an agent turn introduces a voluminous test log or extensive retrieval chunk, it inevitably injects new prefill compute into the execution engine. Scheduling that compute-heavy prefill alongside ongoing, memory-bandwidth-bound token generation for existing requests is the next serving problem.

5.6 Chunked Prefill Scheduling

Prefix reuse eliminates redundant computation on shared prompt prefixes, but new observations inevitably introduce fresh work. When the repair agent submits selected integration-test output, the serving engine must process the uncached suffix of the serialized request before generating the next response token. Other requests may already be generating tokens on the same accelerators. If the engine schedules the entire new prefill as one uninterrupted monolithic batch, those ongoing decode streams must wait for that pass to finish before executing their next decode step. In interactive agent workflows, that stall translates directly into noticeable inter-token latency spikes.

Trimming the observation reduces the raw number of tokens to process, but once the request reaches the server, scheduling determines how execution time is shared among active streams. The engine does not need to execute a long prefill as an all-or-nothing unit. Continuous batching already re-forms the batch at every forward step, admitting and retiring requests between token-generation steps (Yu et al. 2022). That per-iteration boundary is exactly where a slice of one request’s prefill can be combined with other requests’ decodes. By splitting long prefills into smaller token chunks that co-run alongside ongoing decodes, the scheduler bounds how long any single iteration can stall active generation streams (Agrawal et al. 2023, 2024).

Agrawal, Amey, Nitin Kedia, Ashish Panwar, Jayashree Mohan, Nipun Kwatra, Bhargav S. Gulavani, Alexey Tumanov, and Ramachandran Ramjee. 2024. “Taming Throughput-Latency Tradeoff in LLM Inference with Sarathi-Serve.” 18th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation (OSDI 24), 117–34.

↩︎ Prefill, decode, time to first token, and continuous batching are established in Where a call spends its time.

Definition 5.4: Chunked prefill

Chunked prefill is a scheduling policy that splits a request’s uncached prompt into token spans processed over several engine iterations, each iteration also advancing ongoing decodes, while preserving causal order.

  1. Significance: It bounds the gap between consecutive output tokens for ongoing requests to the duration of a single chunked iteration rather than a full monolithic prefill, at the expense of a modest delay in time to first token for the new request.
  2. Distinction: A chunk represents an execution quantum for the scheduler rather than a physical storage layout. The memory allocator maps the resulting KV activations into memory blocks whose page sizes operate independently of chunk boundaries.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating the per-iteration token budget as an exact wall-clock time limit. Iteration latency depends on attended context length, memory bandwidth, and batch composition, so operators must calibrate the budget against measured gaps between output tokens under realistic workloads.

5.6.1 Causal correctness across chunks

Splitting a prompt across several iterations would change the model’s outputs if a later chunk attended only to tokens inside its own slice or used reset position indices. To preserve exact causal attention across chunks, each query token in the active chunk must attend to the compatible per-layer KV state produced by all earlier prefixes. It must also attend to the prior and concurrent tokens within its own chunk, as the causal mask enforces, while remaining blind to subsequent tokens. The engine preserves continuous positional encodings and mask logic as it extends the stored KV state, loading previously computed keys and values from memory rather than recomputing their projections from raw token embeddings. Chunking therefore alters the temporal execution schedule while preserving exact causal semantics, modulo minor floating-point non-associativity across kernel tiling and batching configurations. This scheduling flexibility incurs two physical costs: subsequent chunks must repeatedly stream earlier KV activations from high-bandwidth memory, increasing aggregate memory traffic, and smaller prefill slices introduce additional kernel-launch and scheduling overheads.

5.6.2 The per-iteration token budget

Admitting too many prefill tokens into one iteration stalls active decode streams, while admitting too few stretches a new prompt across dozens of small, overhead-heavy steps. To bound the work of each forward pass, a scheduler establishes an iteration-level token budget \(\IterationTokenBudget\). In standard autoregressive decoding, each scheduled decode request contributes one token position, whereas each scheduled prefill contributes the token count of its next chunk. An engine iteration admitting prefill chunks of size \(\PrefillAllocatedTokenCount_i\) alongside a set \(\DecodeRequestSet\) of active decode requests must satisfy

\[\sum_{i} \PrefillAllocatedTokenCount_i + \lvert\DecodeRequestSet\rvert \le \IterationTokenBudget \tag{3}\]

while ensuring that the admitted combination fits within available accelerator memory. A request whose prompt spans multiple chunks retains its partially computed prefix KV state in memory across iterations while waiting for its subsequent slices to run.

A common scheduling policy prioritizes ongoing decoding requests by reserving \(\lvert\DecodeRequestSet\rvert\) slots first, dedicating the remaining capacity \(\IterationTokenBudget - \lvert\DecodeRequestSet\rvert\) to prefill chunks. This decoding-first priority ensures steady token generation for active streams without requiring them to wait for an entire multi-thousand-token prompt. However, the arriving request may require several iterations to complete its prefill, particularly if heavy decode traffic restricts the available chunk size. Conversely, allocating too much budget to prefill chunks can starve decode streams and trigger latency spikes. A robust scheduler must balance decode responsiveness against prefill completion progress to avoid both stream stalls and prompt starvation.

The weight-only step-time model \(\WeightOnlyStepTime(\ForwardPassTokenCount)\) of the prefix-hit estimate gives the scale of this trade-off, with \(\ForwardPassTokenCount\) the token positions that equation 3 admits in one iteration. With 32 ongoing decodes and nothing else, each step sits at the weight-read floor of about 5 ms. Admitting a 4,096-token prefill as one separate unit inserts about 162 ms of arithmetic before the next decode step runs (weight work only, so slightly below the 169 ms prefix rebuild, which includes attention). Waiting through that prefill pass and the 5 ms decode pass stretches the gap between output tokens to about 168 ms.

Dividing that prompt into 512-token chunks reshapes execution by batching one prefill chunk and the 32 decode tokens into the same forward pass. Each of the eight iterations now carries 544 positions, sits in the arithmetic-bound regime, and takes about 22 ms. Because the decodes advance on every iteration, their worst gap between tokens is one 22 ms step, about 8× smaller than under the monolithic schedule. The new prompt completes after eight such mixed iterations, or about 173 ms instead of 162 ms, because each pass also performs the weight arithmetic for the 32 co-scheduled decode tokens. Chunking thus buys existing streams a much shorter gap for a modest delay in the new request’s first token. On one time axis, the monolithic schedule leaves a single long gap between two output tokens, while the chunked schedule places a token at every iteration boundary and finishes the prompt only slightly later (figure 5). Both schedules omit attention traffic, which grows with each chunk’s context, and per-iteration overhead, so measured iterations run longer and the budget must come from measurement.

Characterizing these trade-offs requires replaying representative arrival traces across candidate token budgets while tracking key systems metrics: time to first token for newly admitted requests, inter-token latency for active decode streams, aggregate token throughput, and accelerator memory occupancy. High-percentile latency measurements, such as the p99 inter-token gap, expose transient scheduling hiccups far better than arithmetic averages, which easily conceal the disruptive stalls of occasional large prefills. Operators must also distinguish server iteration step times from client-perceived streaming intervals; downstream buffering, transport serialization, and network round-trip times introduce variances that server-side metrics do not capture.

Selecting the token budget \(\IterationTokenBudget\) ultimately balances hardware execution efficiency against service responsiveness. Smaller chunks provide frequent scheduling checkpoints that protect inter-token deadlines, yet they extend total prompt completion times and add kernel-launch overhead. Larger chunks maximize tensor-core utilization by providing wider matrix dimensions during attention and feed-forward operations, but they penalize concurrent decode streams with longer stalls. In practice, operators configure the token budget to the largest chunk size whose corresponding iteration duration comfortably satisfies the target inter-token latency objective for active streams. On the reference node, the weight-only estimate gives the starting point. An inter-token target comfortably above 22 ms admits the 512-token chunk alongside 32 decodes. A tighter target forces smaller chunks and a later first token for the new request, while running the prompt unchunked exposes the decodes to the full 168 ms gap. Measured attention traffic and per-iteration overhead then shrink the admissible chunk from that estimate.

Prefill-decode disaggregation, introduced with the call-latency decomposition of Where a call spends its time, takes the structural alternative to chunking and places the two phases on separate worker pools (Patel et al. 2024; Zhong et al. 2024). It replaces compute contention on one worker with a transfer of each prompt’s KV state from the prefill pool to a decode worker before generation starts. When the interconnect saturates or queues build at either pool, that transfer can cost more than phase isolation saves. For an agent calling a hosted endpoint, that topology stays behind the serving API and appears only as request latency, streaming variance, and concurrency limits.

Chunking and disaggregation govern how active requests share compute resources during model execution. Once generation completes and an agent suspends execution to invoke an external tool or inspect an environment, the operational challenge changes from scheduling compute to managing idle memory. Deciding whether that trajectory’s KV state remains resident in accelerator memory, spills to host memory, or evicts entirely becomes the governing concern.

5.7 Paused-State Policies

While the r18 repair request waits 45 seconds for its integration test suite (or whenever an agent blocks on an external tool or remote database), the model generates no tokens, yet retaining its KV cache in accelerator memory ties up scarce HBM. To prevent idle requests from starving active generation, the serving engine must execute a paused-state policy: retain the state in device memory, offload it to host memory or secondary storage, or drop it entirely and reconstruct it when the tool output arrives. Systems research on tool-augmented generation evaluated these same three mechanisms (preserve, swap, and discard), selecting dynamically among them per request to minimize wasted accelerator memory (Abhyankar et al. 2024). This choice balances device memory capacity against host storage, interconnect transfer time, and recomputation latency.

Because the KV pool is a mechanical bound, invariant closure (principle 1) places this lifecycle decision below the model. The model cannot observe pool occupancy, its proposals cannot enforce a memory release, and the correctness of the next turn never depends on where the state was stored during the wait. The serving engine therefore executes the policy and chooses whether to keep, move, or drop the blocks, while the agent runtime may supply hints through the serving interface, such as the expected wait duration or the likelihood that the trajectory will resume.

↪ Harness supervision of resources retained during external tool waits is developed in Resources retained while waiting.

5.7.1 Reclaimable state

Evicting or offloading a paused trajectory frees far less memory than its logical token count suggests whenever its prefix blocks are still shared with active requests or pinned by in-flight transfers. Prefix blocks shared with concurrent trajectories, such as common system prompts or branching execution paths, must remain allocated on the accelerator as long as another request holds a valid reference. Similarly, blocks currently committed to in-flight asynchronous direct memory access (DMA) copies or active kernel operations cannot be freed until those transfers complete. The uniquely reclaimable footprint consists solely of unshared blocks with no pending operations, and every pause calculation must use this private footprint as its baseline.

Offloading state requires available capacity at the target tier and a coordinated handoff protocol. To maintain memory safety, the engine must preserve source blocks in accelerator memory until the outbound DMA transfer to host memory verifies successfully. During the transfer window, the state consumes both device HBM and host DRAM concurrently. The host-memory budget must therefore accommodate these transient transfer buffers alongside already parked trajectories. Upon resumption, the sequence reverses: the scheduler must allocate accelerator blocks, initiate an inward transfer, and update page tables before attention kernels can resume execution.

Discarding the KV cache relinquishes the computed state while preserving the underlying information, provided the raw input sequence and environment observations remain recorded in durable logs. Because attention state is derived from the token prefix (principle 5), state lost this way can be derived again. If the request resumes, the engine reconstructs the missing attention state by re-running prefill across the historical prompt. This reconstruction requires exact provenance: the precise token sequence, active LoRA adapters, position encodings, attention masks, and multimodal embeddings. A compressed conversation summary cannot substitute for the raw token history. Reconstruction typically reuses any compatible prefix blocks that remain in memory and prefills only the missing suffix. In managed inference platforms, callers can exploit these trade-offs only to the degree that the hosting service exposes explicit prefix caching or session controls.

5.7.2 Keep, move, and drop policies

For those uniquely reclaimable blocks, the serving engine has three choices while the tool runs: keep them on the accelerator at the cost of HBM capacity, move them to host memory at the cost of interconnect bandwidth, or drop them and rebuild the suffix through prefill when the tool returns. Table 4 compares how the three policies trade accelerator memory against restoration work.

Each policy targets a distinct operational regime. Retaining state on the accelerator is optimal when the memory pool has ample headroom and the agent anticipates an immediate return. Holding the blocks incurs zero marginal cost if no competing work requires memory, and it guarantees zero restoration latency when generation resumes. Under memory pressure, moving state to host DRAM releases accelerator capacity without discarding the completed prefill computation. Dropping state becomes the preferred choice when the probability of resumption is low, host DRAM is exhausted, or the context is short enough that recomputing prefill is faster than transferring the data across the bus. Whichever policy runs, the engine must enforce memory isolation boundaries so that retained, offloaded, or evicted blocks remain protected from unauthorized access by other tenants.

When pause durations extend across minutes or hours, as for workflows waiting for human approval, the engine can migrate state beyond host DRAM to non-volatile storage. Hierarchical serving systems organize storage across host memory and NVMe drives, prefetching cached layers sequentially so that retrieval from disk overlaps with execution of earlier layers (Gao et al. 2024). At data center scale, distributed architectures pool the host memory and solid-state storage of thousands of nodes into a shared cluster-wide KV cache (Qin et al. 2024). Across all storage tiers, cached attention tensors represent derived, transient state; the durable history of user inputs, tool actions, and execution evidence resides in the runtime’s authoritative event log.

5.7.3 Comparing reload and recompute during the test wait

When the repair agent pauses at r18 for its 45 s integration test on the eight-accelerator node, its 32,768-token context puts concrete numbers on all three policies. With all historical KV entries materialized, every 16-token block completely filled, and no blocks shared or committed to in-flight transfers, the uniquely reclaimable cache footprint is 10.74 GB (excluding auxiliary page tables and transfer buffers, which the runtime budgets separately).

The transfer and recompute durations derive directly from hardware specifications. Because tensor parallelism partitions key-value heads evenly across the eight accelerators, each device holds one-eighth of the total state and transfers data over its own 64 GB/s PCIe link. When all eight links operate concurrently, the node moves state at an aggregate bandwidth of \(\HostLinkBandwidth =\) 512 GB/s. Offloading the complete 10.74 GB payload across this interface requires 21 ms, and restoring it upon resumption takes an identical 21 ms.

Reconstruction instead repeats the prefill pass. For a model with \(\Parameters\) parameters and a sequence of \(\SequenceLength\) tokens, evaluating the feed-forward and projection weights requires \(2\Parameters \SequenceLength\) floating-point operations. At the sustained prefill rate assumed for the reference system, recomputing this context demands 1.3 s. Including the quadratic attention term of equation 4 would lengthen it further. (Because every policy must eventually process the incoming test observation once the wait concludes, that downstream prefill is common to all policies and omitted from this comparison.)

In this weight-only comparison, both transfer duration and recompute duration scale linearly with sequence length \(\SequenceLength\), so their ratio is independent of context size:

\[\frac{\KvReloadTime}{\KvRecomputeTime} = \frac{\SequenceLength \, \KvCacheBytesPerToken / \HostLinkBandwidth}{2 \Parameters \SequenceLength / \AttainableComputeRate} = \frac{\KvCacheBytesPerToken \, \AttainableComputeRate}{2 \Parameters \, \HostLinkBandwidth} \tag{4}\]

Reloading paused state beats rebuilding it by about 62× in the linear reference comparison.

Here \(\AttainableComputeRate\) represents the sustained prefill throughput, and \(\KvCacheBytesPerToken\) is the cache footprint per token. Equation 4 applies the reload-versus-reconstruction comparison of Queueing for Trajectories to this reference architecture. Reloading retained state is approximately 62× faster than recomputing from raw tokens. Even when heavy bus contention degrades link bandwidth to a quarter of its rated capacity, reloading remains 15× faster than prefill. Under this linear reference comparison, recomputation becomes advantageous when host DRAM capacity or PCIe bandwidth is sufficiently constrained by competing workloads, leaving accelerator compute as the less congested resource.

Retaining the cache on-chip throughout the 45 s wait ties up 10.74 GB \(\times\) 45 s \(\approx\) 483.18 GB·s of memory-time capacity (calculated using the unrounded payload). This cumulative occupancy translates into lost serving capacity whenever concurrent requests queue for device memory. Counted in request slots rather than bytes, that occupancy is a residence. Little’s law is the queueing identity that equates mean occupancy with arrival rate times mean residence. It converts this residence into a lost turn rate in section 5.8.2, where the same wait divides the memory-bound rate by the ratio of the whole turn cycle to its generation time.

Moving the state to host memory immediately upon entering the pause releases those accelerator blocks as soon as the 21 ms outbound copy completes. The freed blocks can serve other queries for 45 s \(-\) 21 ms \(=\) 44.98 s before the test returns. The policy incurs 42 ms of total transfer work across the bus, but only the inbound reload sits on the critical path of the resuming query, adding 21 ms of restoration delay. Dropping the state avoids bus transfer entirely, but forces 1.3 s of prefill work onto the critical path before generation can restart.

If the engine can predict the tool completion timestamp accurately, typically from a hint the agent runtime supplies, asynchronous prefetching can schedule the inward transfer to complete precisely as the test finishes. Reserving the destination device blocks at the start of that inbound copy yields an accelerator release window of 45 s \(-\) 21 ms \(-\) 21 ms \(=\) 44.96 s. Prefetching hides restoration latency completely while maintaining both transfer stages, though it requires reliable advance knowledge of return times.

The same reasoning extends to every context length and wait.
