The round-trip transfer time, which scales linearly with context length and equals 42 ms in this configuration, sets a necessary condition. A pause shorter than the round trip ends before offloading can move the state out and back without delaying resumption. Keeping it avoids restoration work when memory permits; under pressure, moving or dropping it may still be necessary. Above the round trip, offloading becomes possible, and whether it pays depends on whether other requests are queued for the freed memory and how much reload delay the resuming turn tolerates. For long pauses under memory pressure, offloading reclaims device memory for almost the entire wait at the cost of a sub-second reload. The worked 32,768-token, 45 s test sits far beyond that round trip. It also sits short of the much longer waits at which parked state accumulates enough to fill host memory, a boundary set by host capacity and by how fast paused trajectories arrive rather than by a fixed duration. The example therefore falls squarely in the offload regime (figure 6). When pauses stretch into minutes or hours, as for operations awaiting human review, parked state accumulates in host memory, and the decision turns on host capacity, secondary storage bandwidth, or eventual recomputation.

5.7.4 Uncertain returns and competing transfers

External tool runs vary widely in duration: a test suite may fail immediately on a syntax error, finish early, or stall on an environmental hang, and this variance complicates offload and prefetch decisions. If a tool completes earlier than forecasted while offloading is still in flight, the engine must either wait for the outbound transfer to finalize before reloading or cancel the transfer mid-stream, exposing unbuffered restoration latency. Conversely, premature prefetching followed by a delayed return re-occupies accelerator memory while the model remains idle, negating the capacity reclaimed during the pause. If a paused trajectory is canceled or times out, an anticipatory inward reload wastes interconnect bandwidth outright. A robust pause policy must therefore evaluate the full probability distribution of return times alongside client latency service-level objectives (SLOs), rather than relying on mean wait times and uncontended transfer estimates.

Furthermore, transfers rarely occur in isolation. Multiple paused trajectories frequently finish their external tasks in close temporal proximity, triggering a surge of concurrent reloads across shared host-memory buses, PCIe switches, and storage links. Summing nominal per-accelerator link rates is valid only if interconnect topologies and memory controllers maintain non-blocking throughput under concurrent load.

Suppose 32 paused repairs on a node finish their external tasks simultaneously. Their combined attention state totals 344 GB, which requires at least 0.67 s to reload even when the eight host links sustain their maximum aggregate throughput. The final reload in the queue therefore experiences a restoration delay roughly 32× longer than the isolated 21 ms reload latency, and any congestion on shared bus root complexes lengthens the tail further. System engineers must provision transfer queues, admission controls, and accelerator headroom specifically to absorb these burst-induced restoration delays.

Pipelining can overlap data transfers with computation, but strict causal dependencies dictate which operations can run concurrently. In standard full-attention models, generating or processing new observation tokens requires access to all historical key and value tensors. An engine cannot compute attention for layer \(\LayerIndex\) until the historical KV blocks for layer \(\LayerIndex\) reside in accelerator memory. Transfer latency can therefore be hidden only if the engine streams state in layer by layer (reloading layer \(\LayerIndex+1\) while executing attention on layer \(\LayerIndex\)) or if the node schedules unrelated generation requests while the paused state reloads in the background. Both techniques require careful engine coordination: layer-wise streaming requires fine-grained synchronization across DMA engines, while concurrent execution requires reserve accelerator memory to host the in-flight requests alongside incoming reload buffers.

5.7.5 Reclamation vs. approximation

Dropping individual tokens from an active KV cache or quantizing its keys and values shrinks memory occupancy without waiting for a tool pause. Unlike keeping, offloading, or recomputing, however, these lossy approximations alter the model’s forward pass and can silently break a multi-turn repair when an evicted token turns out to matter ten turns later. Because selective token eviction, local sliding windows, and low-precision KV quantization change the underlying model computation, their efficacy cannot be judged by memory savings alone; they require empirical validation against task accuracy. In agentic workflows, an eviction policy cannot assume that low attention weight during past decoding steps implies irrelevance in future turns.

The mechanics of attention sinks demonstrate why naive eviction schemes break down. Because softmax normalizes attention weights to sum to one across the sequence, transformer layers deposit residual attention mass on the initial sequence positions whenever a query has no strong semantic match among current tokens. A sliding-window policy that evicts those initial tokens removes this stabilizing mass, causing generation perplexity to degrade sharply once context length exceeds the window size. Retaining a small set of initial sink tokens alongside the sliding window restores numerical stability across long sequence streams (Xiao et al. 2024). More sophisticated pruning strategies retain tokens that accumulate high cumulative attention mass (Zhang et al. 2023) or that receive significant attention within a recent temporal window (Liu et al. 2023).

Liu, Zichang, Aditya Desai, Fangshuo Liao, Weitao Wang, Victor Xie, Zhaozhuo Xu, Anastasia Mazur, Chen Dun, and Anshumali Shrivastava. 2023. “Scissorhands: Exploiting the Persistence of Importance Hypothesis for LLM KV Cache Compression.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS).

While past attention predicts future utility reasonably well in conventional conversation, agent trajectories routinely violate this assumption in three distinct ways:

  1. Dormant constraints: Instructions defined early in an execution trace (such as environment constraints, security policies, or API rules) often receive negligible attention across dozens of intermediate tool turns. When the agent produces its final edit or synthesis, violating those dormant instructions causes silent task failure.
  2. Exact identifiers: Unique entities like commit hashes, process IDs, file paths, and cryptographic nonces may appear once during environment exploration and remain unreferenced for many turns. If an eviction heuristic prunes these tokens because intermediate turns ignored them, the agent cannot quote them accurately in subsequent shell or API calls.
  3. Tool-schema syntax: Schema definitions and JSON delimiters in the system prompt govern the grammar of every tool call. Individual decoding steps frequently assign weak attention mass to structural delimiters like brackets and colons. Pruning them produces malformed syntax that crashes downstream parsers rather than a graceful degradation in output quality.

Cache approximation in agentic systems must therefore be evaluated against hard semantic dependencies rather than aggregate perplexity. The lossless policies (keep, move, and drop) cost interconnect bandwidth and tiered memory capacity, but they preserve the full-attention computation for the same recorded inputs and compatible configuration, subject to numerical differences across implementations. They decide where one paused trajectory’s state lives. They leave open how many trajectories a node can carry when each one holds memory for its whole residence, waits included, and answering that requires putting time into the capacity accounting.

5.8 Capacity for Agent Workloads

A paused repair task can hold key-value state in accelerator memory for minutes without executing a single forward pass, while an incoming request demands both memory capacity and execution time immediately. How an engine handles paused state dictates how many concurrent agent trajectories can fit in memory. Converting that static concurrency into a sustainable throughput, however, requires modeling how long each session holds its memory. Capacity planning therefore first establishes physical occupancy bounds and then couples those bounds to residence time.

Tracking memory demand in agentic serving differs from tracking standard batch inference, where every active sequence holds a private cache. In an agent workload, logical contexts overlap through shared system prompts and tool descriptions while continuations allocate private blocks and dormant prefixes wait to be reused or paged out. Summing the logical key-value footprints of all active trajectories therefore double-counts shared prefix blocks, whereas counting only the requests currently decoding tokens ignores the dormant KV state that still occupies HBM. Capacity planning must instead count unique physical blocks across the pool.

5.8.1 A resident-fit bound

Sizing a node by dividing raw HBM by a prompt’s token footprint overestimates how many requests can safely run at once. This happens because each admitted request also needs room for its generated output, rounds its allocation to whole blocks, and shares part of the pool with runtime headroom. To build a capacity model that accounts for these bounds, consider a set of equal-length requests with input prompt length \(\SequenceLength\) and a maximum generation allowance of \(\OutputLimit\) tokens. Because paged allocation hands out fixed-size blocks of \(\KvBlockSize\) tokens, each private allocation rounds up to the nearest whole block. Without prefix sharing, every resident request must independently provision memory for its full prompt and output allowance. When requests share an identical, compatible prefix, however, the memory pool allocates those prefix blocks once and pays only for each request’s private remainder. For this simple decomposition, assume that the common prefix aligns cleanly with a block boundary; any trailing partial block follows the copy-on-write accounting described earlier.

The memory available for key-value caching is whatever high-bandwidth memory remains after allocating model weights and reserving memory for execution runtimes:

\[\KvPoolMemory = \NodeHbmCapacity - \WeightMemory - \RuntimeReserveMemory \tag{5}\]

Production deployments typically withhold an additional headroom fraction \(\KvPoolHeadroomFraction\) of this pool to absorb bursty intermediate activations and memory fragmentation. Given block size \(\KvBlockSize\), a block-aligned common prefix of \(\SharedPrefixLength\) tokens, and a cache footprint of \(\KvCacheBytesPerToken\) bytes per token across all layers, the total physical block demand across \(\BatchSize\) resident requests (every request whose state is resident, whether decoding or paused) must satisfy

\[\left\lceil \frac{\SharedPrefixLength}{\KvBlockSize} \right\rceil + \BatchSize \left\lceil \frac{\SequenceLength + \OutputLimit - \SharedPrefixLength}{\KvBlockSize} \right\rceil \le \left\lfloor \frac{(1-\KvPoolHeadroomFraction)\, \KvPoolMemory}{\KvBlockSize \, \KvCacheBytesPerToken} \right\rfloor \tag{6}\]

The resident-fit bound of section 5.2, refined for blocks, headroom, and sharing, is the maximum integer \(\BatchSize\) that satisfies equation 6. Setting \(\SharedPrefixLength = 0\) yields the unshared baseline. Let \(\SharedPrefixFraction\) denote the fraction of a full request’s blocks that belong to the shared prefix, so each additional request allocates only the private fraction \(1 - \SharedPrefixFraction\) of an unshared request’s blocks. When the usable pool is large compared with the single copy of the shared prefix, paying for that prefix once leaves almost the entire pool for private blocks, and dividing by \(1 - \SharedPrefixFraction\) per request raises the resident fit by nearly \(1/(1 - \SharedPrefixFraction)\). Sharing therefore delivers its largest gains when common instructions and workspace state represent a dominant fraction of the total context and private working memory (the unique turn history and generation allowance) remains small. Notebook 5.3 grounds these formulations on the same eight-accelerator DGX H100 node serving Llama 3 70B. Rather than optimistically assuming an average generation length, the sizing calculation reserves the full \(\OutputLimit\) token allowance, which guarantees that resident requests cannot trigger out-of-memory aborts during decoding. It treats the headroom fraction as a deliberate provisioning reserve.

Napkin Math 5.3: From logical contexts to physical fit
Problem: How many complete request snapshots fit when a common prefix is stored once rather than copied for every request?

Variables: The Llama 3 70B model uses 16-bit weights and KV state on the DGX H100 node. After weights and the stipulated 20 GB runtime reserve, its KV pool (equation 5) is 526.0 GB. Withhold an additional 10 percent. Each request has 32,768 input tokens and an allowance of 2,048 output tokens. The requests come from a fleet of repair workers on one repository snapshot, so their first 24,576 input tokens (instructions, tool schemas, and the shared workspace state) are identical and form the common prefix. Blocks hold 16 tokens, or 5.243 MB of KV state across the node.

Math: Divide the remaining pool by bytes per block and round down, giving 90,292 usable blocks. An unshared request occupies 2,176 blocks (11.41 GB). Dividing the usable block count by that request count and rounding down gives 41 resident requests. That count is down from the 48 of table 1 because each request also reserves its output allowance, the headroom is withheld, and allocation rounds to whole blocks. With sharing, subtract the common prefix’s 1,536 blocks (8.05 GB) once, then divide by 640 private blocks per request (3.36 GB). Rounding down gives 138 resident requests.

Systems insight: The common prefix covers 70.6 percent of each request’s blocks, and sharing it raises the fit 3.4×, close to the large-pool limit \(1/(1-\SharedPrefixFraction) \approx\) 3.4× for a shared fraction \(\SharedPrefixFraction\). In that limit, the gain depends on \(\SharedPrefixFraction\) alone, making prompt layout and the scope of sharing the primary architectural levers. A prefix shared only across one deployment’s instructions covers far less of each request than one that also spans a common workspace, as the archetype comparison below shows. Within a given scope, keeping invariant tokens at the front of the serialized input, strictly ahead of any volatile turn history, maximizes \(\SharedPrefixFraction\). Although sharing does not reduce the decoding work per token or change any request’s logical context, it multiplies concurrent capacity. These values are physical fit bounds for the designated memory partition, not runtime admission limits.

The sharing gain \(1/(1-\SharedPrefixFraction)\) steepens as the shared fraction of each request grows.

In production environments, requests arrive with varied lengths and different degrees of prefix overlap. An admission controller must therefore balance committed blocks, anticipated generation growth, and temporary staging buffers required during KV transfers or eviction copies. Furthermore, an aggregate node-wide memory bound is necessary but not sufficient: multi-GPU deployments distribute KV cache blocks across tensor-parallel shards, meaning a free physical block on one accelerator cannot alleviate memory exhaustion on another. Admission decisions must satisfy physical capacity constraints across every individual device.

5.8.2 Residence time and service rate

A node that fits 41 unshared agent contexts in memory at once can still sustain fewer than one turn per second if every slot stays occupied across a 45 s integration test. A static fit bound counts only how many slots can exist in memory simultaneously. Translating that capacity into sustainable throughput requires dividing by how long each request holds its slot, and classical queueing theory supplies the identity that links capacity, residence, and throughput.

Theorem 5.1: Occupancy equals rate times residence (Little's law)
In any stable system where items arrive, reside for a while, and leave, the long-run averages obey one identity, whatever the arrival pattern, service order, or residence distribution. Little’s law (Little 1961) states

\[\MeanSlotOccupancy = \ArrivalRate \MeanHoldTime \tag{7}\]

  • Mean occupancy (\(\MeanSlotOccupancy\)): average number of items resident, here KV slots held.
  • Throughput (\(\ArrivalRate\)): long-run arrival rate, equal to the completion rate when unfinished work does not accumulate.
  • Mean residence (\(\MeanHoldTime\)): average time an item holds its slot, counting every phase in which the slot stays allocated.

A slot held in accelerator memory while an agent waits for external tool execution remains resident throughout that wait; omitting idle waiting time from \(\MeanHoldTime\) artificially deflates the denominator and overstates sustainable system throughput.

The law’s generality has a price. Its proof assumes nothing about how items arrive or how long they stay, only that the long-run averages exist, and for that reason it fixes the mean occupancy and says nothing about the peak. A burst of arrivals, or residences that end together, can hold far more slots than \(\MeanSlotOccupancy\) for as long as the backlog lasts, so capacity is sized from the arrival and residence patterns, not from the mean identity alone.

Little, John D. C. 1961. “A Proof for the Queuing Formula: \(L = \lambda W\).” Operations Research 9 (3): 383–87. https://doi.org/10.1287/opre.9.3.383.

Rearranging the relation as \(\ArrivalRate = \MeanSlotOccupancy/\MeanHoldTime\) expresses the achievable service rate as a function of allocated capacity and residence duration. Here each resident item is one turn’s generation-and-tool-wait cycle, not a complete multi-turn trajectory. Let \(\ModelTime\) be the time a turn spends generating and \(\ToolTime\) the time it then waits on its tool. A slot kept through the wait resides for \(\ModelTime + \ToolTime\), while a slot released for the wait resides for \(\ModelTime\) alone. For the same number of slots, the two policies’ turn rates therefore differ by the factor \(1 + \ToolTime/\ModelTime\). That factor is the residency tax of a synchronous tool wait, and notebook 5.4 prices it for the repair turn.

↪ Little’s law applied to whole multi-turn trajectories with tool waits is developed in Queueing for Trajectories.

Napkin Math 5.4: The residency tax of a tool wait
Problem: The node holds 41 unshared request slots. Each repair turn resides 5 s while generating and, under the keep policy, another 45 s while its integration test runs. What turn rate can memory sustain with the wait held resident, and with the slot released for the wait?

Math: By equation 7, \(\ArrivalRate = \MeanSlotOccupancy/\MeanHoldTime\). Keeping state through the wait gives \(\MeanHoldTime =\) 50 s and \(\ArrivalRate =\) 0.82 turns/s. Releasing the slot for the wait gives \(\MeanHoldTime =\) 5 s and \(\ArrivalRate =\) 8.2 turns/s. The ratio is \(1 + \ToolTime/\ModelTime\), or 10×.

Systems insight: Holding paused state through an idle tool wait penalizes the memory-bound turn rate by a factor of \(1 + \ToolTime/\ModelTime\). The economic viability of keeping state in accelerator memory depends on the ratio of wait duration to generation time, not the wait duration alone. Once the wait dominates, moving the state out to host memory, at a restoration latency of about 21 ms, becomes the better default whenever memory is contested. This same factor scales the memory term of every attempt’s cost, expanding \(\mathbb{E}[\AttemptCost]\) in equation 1.2 across the entire unevicted residence.

A kept slot is paid for across the whole cycle, rather than only while it generates.

The turn rate calculated from releasing slots is an ideal upper bound imposed strictly by memory capacity. In practice, prefill computation, state restoration bandwidth, PCIe bus contention, and scheduling queues introduce additional bottlenecks; compute throughput or interconnect bandwidth often saturates before memory is fully exhausted. Furthermore, if multiple trajectories share prefix blocks, those blocks remain pinned in memory even if one trajectory enters an idle wait.

Because Little’s law fixes only the mean, the residency calculation also hides the peaks that agent workloads produce. Tool executions frequently complete together, as in the correlated return burst of section 5.7.4, and tree-search agents fan out into many candidate paths at once, so instantaneous memory demand rises well above the average for as long as the burst lasts. Robust capacity planning must therefore stress-test the empirical distributions of prompt length, dynamic output expansion, search branching, and tool latencies against both device-level physical boundaries and strict service-level latency objectives.

These dynamics manifest differently across agent architectures. The software repair agent operates in a batch-like regime characterized by deep context windows and prolonged tool waits during test suite runs. By contrast, the customer support agent services numerous concurrent, short sessions where users wait interactively for low-latency responses. These contrasting profiles place divergent demands on the key-value pool and require distinct sharing and retention strategies.

Lighthouse 5.1: Attention-state residency by archetype
Table 5 puts the repair agent beside a support agent on the same node and pool. Both deployments share a 6,144-token prefix of system prompt and tool schemas across all their sessions. That prefix carries no customer or repository data, so isolation permits sharing it across sessions. The scope is narrower than in notebook 5.3, where repair workers on one repository snapshot also share their workspace state. The support agent’s inputs are scenario values, not measurements.

Systems insight: Prefix sharing yields asymmetric benefits across these two archetypes. Sharing the common prompt prefix expands the support agent’s resident session capacity 4×, yet yields only a 1.25× expansion for the repair agent, because the shared prefix constitutes a far larger fraction of each support session’s total context. Repair sessions recover a large sharing gain only when the scope widens to a common workspace, which raised the fit 3.4× in the capacity notebook. While both workloads pay a similar residency tax, the optimal pause policy diverges based on who waits for the resume. The repair agent should migrate its state to host memory during tests, as the regime analysis of figure 6 recommends. It can fall back to dropping the state when host memory is tight, since recomputation adds only 2.6 percent to a 50 s turn cycle where an automated test harness waits. In contrast, the support agent should keep or move its state and avoid dropping it, because recomputation lands directly in front of an interactive user as added delay to the first token. For support workloads, the decision that matters most is therefore what happens to each session’s private history beyond the shared prefix. Rebuilding that history is what the customer waits for, so keeping or moving it, not enlarging the shared prefix, protects the first token.

5.8.3 KV pool telemetry

A single aggregate metric such as occupied pool fraction cannot distinguish healthy, amortized caching from an impending allocation failure. A memory pool at high utilization composed mostly of discardable dormant prefixes can easily admit a new request through cheap reclamation. Conversely, that same utilization level with all blocks pinned by active decoding leaves no headroom for context expansion. Table 6 establishes the physical categories required to interpret these operational states.

Evaluating blocks in this strict order ensures that every physical block belongs to exactly one state at any given instant. For instance, while a paused trajectory’s private blocks are being copied out to host memory, each source block stays in the first row, pinned by the transfer that still reads it, and becomes reclaimable only when the copy completes. On the return trip, the device blocks allocated to receive the reload sit in the second row until the transfer lands. Similarly, a dormant prefix is reclaimable only when all dependent references and asynchronous operations have cleared. This taxonomy exposes true admission capacity without double-counting shared blocks or assuming that eviction requests free memory instantaneously.

Physical pool accounting provides only the static half of the operational picture; it must be coupled with continuous telemetry on throughput, work volume, and latency. A production telemetry pipeline should record offered request rates, prompt and completion token volumes, the token-weighted prefix hit rate of equation 2, eviction and restoration bandwidth, recomputed tokens, and the distribution of tool wait and resumption intervals. Operators must evaluate these metrics alongside concrete latency targets: admission delay in the scheduling queue, time to first token, inter-token latency, and state resumption delay. Hit rates in isolation can mislead. A high reuse fraction can coexist with long scheduling queues, while high eviction counts may be entirely benign if the discarded prefixes represent cold sessions that will never be queried again.

The definitive signature of memory thrashing is cache churn: the rapid cycle of evicting blocks only to reload or recompute them shortly thereafter.8 When cache churn coincides with degrading tail latency or falling completed work under sustained offered load, it signals that the aggregate working set of active trajectories has overrun physical memory capacity. Resolving this collapse requires adjusting admission policies, offload thresholds, or scheduling priorities. Because the memory occupancy threshold at which churn begins varies across allocators and workload compositions, operators must correlate physical pool states with queue dynamics rather than relying on a static utilization ceiling. Furthermore, this diagnostic boundary matters across system layers: client applications consuming remote API endpoints observe only black-box latency and error codes, whereas infrastructure operators have access to the physical block states, page tables, and transfer telemetry required to perform root-cause remediation. Coupling resident-fit bounds with telemetry across those block states lets operators see which KV blocks must stay pinned, which can be reclaimed or offloaded, and what restoration delay each choice adds to end-to-end execution.

8 Thrashing: Multiprogrammed virtual-memory systems of the 1960s encountered this exact pathology: when the aggregate working sets of resident programs exceeded main memory, page faults forced continuous eviction of recently used pages, and processors spent their execution cycles servicing I/O rather than executing instructions (Denning 1968b). The solution was explicit load control: restricting the multiprogramming level until the working sets of admitted programs fit comfortably in physical memory. In agentic KV cache serving, the corresponding remedy is admission control against the retained state of active trajectories, rather than tuning eviction algorithms to page faster.

Denning, Peter J. 1968b. “Thrashing: Its Causes and Prevention.” Proceedings of the Fall Joint Computer Conference, Part i (AFIPS ’68), 915–22. https://doi.org/10.1145/1476589.1476705.
Checkpoint 5.2: Fit, pressure, and completed work

Two runs report the same occupied fraction, but one holds mostly dormant prefixes and the other holds mostly active state.

5.9 Fallacies and Pitfalls

Most KV-cache failures in production agent deployments come from two mistakes. The first is treating an instantaneous memory-fit count as a throughput guarantee while idle tool waits hold slots for tens of seconds. The second is treating a fast prefix-cache hit as proof that the underlying environment state is still current. Each fallacy and pitfall below pairs the failure mode with its concrete cost on the reference node.

Fallacy: Model size, not retained context, decides how many sequences a node can serve.

Static model weights consume a fixed footprint allocated at initialization, leaving a 526.0 GB pool dedicated to dynamic attention state on the reference node. While model parameters establish the initial baseline headroom, the active context length of running sequences governs dynamic memory consumption. Holding the model constant, that memory pool accommodates 195 unshared sequences when context is kept to 8,192 tokens, but capacity plunges to just 12 concurrent sequences once context expands to 131,072 tokens (section 5.2). Retained context length serves as the primary operational lever governing system concurrency, and the agent harness dictates that length through its prompt construction, tool history, and conversation buffering. Swapping in a smaller model only increases concurrent serving capacity to the extent that it reduces the per-token memory footprint \(\KvCacheBytesPerToken\) or frees up additional accelerator memory for the KV pool.

Pitfall: Reserving every request’s maximum context.

Allocating contiguous memory upfront for each request’s worst-case context length severely throttles system throughput through internal fragmentation. When a serving system sizes every slot for a 32,768-token cap while requests average a final length of only 2,048 tokens, barely 6.25 percent of the allocated memory actually holds valid attention state upon completion. Under this static reservation regime, the reference pool can admit only 48 concurrent sequences. In contrast, paged virtual memory allocation dynamically allocates physical blocks on demand, giving the final-length snapshot estimate of approximately 780 over the same physical memory (section 5.4). Systems should allocate memory blocks incrementally as tokens arrive during prefill and generation, reserving the output allowance the admission controller promises, as the capacity notebook does, rather than the full context cap.

Fallacy: Compatible cached attention state proves that its evidence is current.

Prefix caching operates strictly at the level of token identifiers and model configurations, without semantic awareness of environment state or external reality. On the reference node, a prefix match on the agent repair loop’s 4,096-token invariant prompt saves roughly 169 ms of prefill computation per turn (section 5.5). The inference engine achieves that speedup regardless of whether those cached tokens reflect stale diagnostics from revision 17 (r17) or up-to-date observations from the current repository source. Prefix match compatibility only confirms that the token sequence and attention architecture match bit-for-bit. Determining whether an earlier r17 failure supports a claim about revision 18 (r18) requires semantic validation in the agent harness before serializing the prompt. Updating the prompt with fresh r18 observations breaks prefix reuse at the first modified token and forfeits part of that computational saving. That forfeited saving is the correct price of grounding the next decision in current evidence (principle 6). Under that principle, no cached, staged, or derived copy supersedes the designated source for the claim it represents, which here is the repository at r18.

↪ Every staged or cached claim is validated against a designated source of record, whose role is developed in Record roles.

Pitfall: Always moving paused state out of accelerator memory.

Blindly offloading KV cache blocks to host memory whenever an agent pauses to execute an external tool wastes interconnect bandwidth and inflates resumption latency. Evacuating the 10.74 GB attention state for a paused repair agent requires 42 ms of link transfer, which adds 21 ms of overhead before the model can resume generation. When the memory pool contains plenty of free blocks, this offload latency provides zero benefit to the system. Conversely, under heavy contention, offloading that same state frees valuable high-bandwidth accelerator memory for 44.98 s across an extended 45 s tool invocation (section 5.7). Serving schedulers must evaluate eviction dynamically based on uniquely reclaimable blocks, active queue pressure, and expected resumption deadlines. An external tool call marks an opportunity for scheduling decisions rather than an automatic eviction trigger.

Fallacy: A resident-fit count is a throughput guarantee.

Calculating how many concurrent request snapshots fit into physical accelerator memory establishes an instantaneous capacity limit, not a sustained throughput guarantee. The reference node fits 41 unshared repair trajectories in memory simultaneously. However, if those sessions hold their memory allocations across an idle 45 s tool pause, the node sustains a maximum of only 0.82 turns/s from memory alone. If the engine instead releases those memory slots during the pause, the memory-only throughput bound rises to 8.2 turns/s (notebook 5.4). Memory fit dictates maximum concurrency (\(\MeanSlotOccupancy\)), but Little’s law (\(\MeanSlotOccupancy = \ArrivalRate \MeanHoldTime\)) dictates that arrival throughput (\(\ArrivalRate\)) is governed equally by mean residence time (\(\MeanHoldTime\)). When agent turns involve long execution pauses or queueing delays, residence time inflates and throughput collapses even though memory looks full. Occupancy stays high, but most of the resident state is idle, so the turn rate is low. Schedulers must validate admission against offered traffic distributions, burst arrivals, and latency targets rather than relying on static slot counts.

Pitfall: Treating KV storage as the authoritative task record.

The KV cache is a hardware-bound optimization artifact, not a durable system of record. Dropping the 10.74 GB repair state from accelerator memory costs approximately 1.3 s of prefill compute to rebuild from raw tokens, and it forfeits zero evidence needed by the task. Retaining or serializing KV cache tensors preserves only intermediate, activation-level computation tied to a specific hardware layout, model architecture, and numerical precision. It does not record the authoritative code revisions, environment observations, or validation evidence that govern an agent’s task progression. Systems must persist trajectory history, tool outputs, and verification state in a durable application runtime. The KV cache should always be treated as an ephemeral, recomputable cache over that authoritative token log, reproducible up to floating-point differences across distinct implementations.

5.10 Summary

Retained key-value (KV) attention state is a cache of intermediate computation. Like any cache, its cost has two parts, capacity and residency. Capacity is the bytes it occupies in high-bandwidth memory, which grow with the model’s tensor geometry and the active sequence length. Allocation decides how many of those bytes do useful work, since coarse blocks strand bytes in internal fragmentation and branches that do not share a common prefix store it twice. Residency is how long a sequence holds those bytes. It is what turns capacity into throughput, because an agent blocked on a tool call still occupies accelerator memory, and every byte it holds is a byte another request cannot use. Every memory management policy examined in this chapter (paged block tables, tree-based prefix eviction, offloading, and recomputation) trades memory footprint against recomputation work and scheduling delay. Cache mechanisms manage physical storage, but they cannot assess whether cached tokens remain semantically valid; context validity stays the responsibility of the agent harness and the context it supplies. Table 7 summarizes the primary analytical relations used throughout this chapter to size, share, schedule, and reclaim key-value cache storage.

Key Takeaways: Managing derived attention state
  • Retained length is the capacity knob: With model weights occupying a static allocation, resident capacity scales inversely with retained sequence length, falling from 195 sequences at 8,192 tokens down to 12 sequences at 131,072 tokens on the reference node. A context length that easily satisfies the model architecture’s maximum context window can still quickly exhaust the serving node’s physical memory pool.
  • Physical blocks, not logical references, consume capacity: Paged attention rounds private sequence state up to fixed-size blocks, whereas identical prefix sequences share physical blocks through reference counting. Reclaiming a finished trajectory frees only those physical blocks whose reference count drops to zero, ensuring active sequences and asynchronous transfer operations remain protected.
  • Prefix reuse requires both structural compatibility and memory availability: Matching token prefixes permit KV cache reuse only when the corresponding physical blocks remain resident and accessible under tenant-isolation rules. A cache hit guarantees neither the freshness of underlying external data nor the semantic validity of the trajectory; preserving cache affinity must never prevent an agent harness from invalidating stale context.
  • Retention policies and compute schedulers manage complementary resources: Chunked prefill schedules incoming prompt tokens alongside active decodes to prevent high latency spikes during token generation. Similarly, each way of handling a paused agent trajectory (retaining its blocks in accelerator memory, offloading them to host RAM or secondary storage, or recomputing them on demand) exchanges memory footprint for interconnect bandwidth and resumption latency. The optimal strategy depends on the link bandwidth and the expected wait duration.
  • Idle tool waits impose a severe residency tax on throughput: Static resident-fit bounds govern instantaneous occupancy rather than sustained request throughput. Holding physical cache blocks resident while an agent waits on external tools or human feedback depresses sustainable turn throughput by the residency tax factor \(1 + \ToolTime/\ModelTime\). Production admission and eviction policies must therefore optimize for end-to-end task completion rates and tail latency rather than raw accelerator memory utilization.

Attention state derived from the token prefix (principle 5) enables the serving engine to reuse previous computation rather than evaluating redundant prompt tokens. Precise block tracking and residency accounting quantify the trade-off between retaining that state and freeing high-bandwidth memory for active generation. Throughout these optimizations, authority stays with the source (principle 6): an inexpensive continuation never justifies serving an obsolete observation when the underlying system or environment has changed.

5.11 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

Almost every memory management technique in this chapter resolves an architectural problem that time-sharing operating systems encountered decades ago. When early programs outgrew physical core memory, the Manchester Atlas one-level store introduced paging (Kilburn et al. 1962). An address translation table mapped a program’s contiguous virtual address space onto scattered physical page frames, freeing programmers from managing manual overlays and allowing memory to allocate without contiguous physical reservations. Decoupling virtual addresses from physical layout promptly exposed new systems trade-offs. Randell (1969) formalized the distinction between internal fragmentation (storage lost inside rounded-up allocations) and external fragmentation, where free memory sits stranded between allocations. Belady (1966) demonstrated that optimal offline page replacement requires clairvoyant knowledge of future memory references, establishing that practical online policies must rely on heuristics. When memory pressure mounted, time-sharing systems routinely collapsed into thrashing, spending virtually all cycles swapping pages rather than executing user code. Denning’s working-set model clarified the dynamics of this failure and proved that the only dependable remedy was admission control. That meant admitting fewer concurrent processes rather than swapping pages more aggressively (Denning 1968a, 1968b). Operating systems also pioneered fine-grained memory sharing. The TENEX system introduced copy-on-write sharing so that multiple processes could reference identical pages safely until one attempted a mutation (Bobrow et al. 1972), while Morrison’s PATRICIA trees indexed strings through shared prefixes (Morrison 1968). Denning (1970) gathered these foundations into the virtual-memory discipline that computer systems curricula teach to this day.

Kilburn, Tom, David B. G. Edwards, Michael J. Lanigan, and Frank H. Sumner. 1962. “One-Level Storage System.” IRE Transactions on Electronic Computers EC-11 (2): 223–35. https://doi.org/10.1109/TEC.1962.5219356.
Vaswani, Ashish, Noam Shazeer, Niki Parmar, Jakob Uszkoreit, Llion Jones, Aidan N. Gomez, Lukasz Kaiser, and Illia Polosukhin. 2017. “Attention Is All You Need.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 30: 5998–6008.

Modern inference serving collided with these identical capacity limits when the autoregressive Transformer architecture made each generated token attend to key and value tensors from all prior tokens (Vaswani et al. 2017). Early efforts mitigated this growth by modifying the neural network architecture itself. Multi-query attention (Shazeer 2019) and grouped-query attention (Ainslie et al. 2023) reduced the memory footprint by sharing key-value heads across multiple query heads. In parallel, Pope et al. (2023) developed an analytical framework showing that accelerator memory capacity and memory bandwidth, rather than floating-point compute throughput, impose the primary bottleneck on context length and batch concurrency.

Subsequent innovations in serving systems recapitulated the operating-systems lineage directly. Orca introduced iteration-level scheduling, enabling fine-grained preemption and dynamic batching where requests join and leave running batches on individual token boundaries (Yu et al. 2022). PagedAttention adapted classical virtual paging directly to key-value cache management, using block tables and copy-on-write mechanisms to eliminate external fragmentation and reclaim the memory previously wasted by reserving contiguous worst-case sequence lengths (Kwon et al. 2023). SGLang introduced radix-tree prefix caching with leaf-first eviction, enabling structural cache reuse across independent requests that share common system prompts or few-shot exemplars (Zheng et al. 2024). To prevent lengthy prompt ingestions from stalling generation, Sarathi introduced chunked prefill, bounding the latency spikes that prefill operations impose on active decode iterations (Agrawal et al. 2023). Finally, DistServe and Splitwise extended this decoupling across physical hardware nodes through phase disaggregation, placing the prefill and decode stages on separate, specialized worker pools (Patel et al. 2024; Zhong et al. 2024).

Yu, Gyeong-In, Joo Seong Jeong, Geon-Woo Kim, Soojeong Kim, and Byung-Gon Chun. 2022. “Orca: A Distributed Serving System for Transformer-Based Generative Models.” 16th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation (OSDI 22), 521–38.
Agrawal, Amey, Ashish Panwar, Jayashree Mohan, Nipun Kwatra, Bhargav S. Gulavani, and Ramachandran Ramjee. 2023. “SARATHI: Efficient LLM Inference by Piggybacking Decodes with Chunked Prefills.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2308.16369, August.
Patel, Pratyush, Esha Choukse, Chaojie Zhang, Aashaka Shah, Íñigo Goiri, Saeed Maleki, and Ricardo Bianchini. 2024. “Splitwise: Efficient Generative LLM Inference Using Phase Splitting.” 2024 ACM/IEEE 51st Annual International Symposium on Computer Architecture (ISCA), 118–32. https://doi.org/10.1109/isca59077.2024.00019.
Zhong, Yinmin, Shengyu Liu, Junda Chen, Jianbo Hu, Yibo Zhu, Xuanzhe Liu, Xin Jin, and Hao Zhang. 2024. “DistServe: Disaggregating Prefill and Decoding for Goodput-Optimized Large Language Model Serving.” 18th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation (OSDI 24), July, 193–210.
Gao, Bin, Zhuomin He, Puru Sharma, Qingxuan Kang, Djordje Jevdjic, Junbo Deng, Xingkun Yang, Zhou Yu, and Pengfei Zuo. 2024. “Cost-Efficient Large Language Model Serving for Multi-Turn Conversations with CachedAttention.” 2024 USENIX Annual Technical Conference (USENIX ATC 24), 111–26.
Qin, Yeqi, Yufan Shen, Zeyu Shi, Chen Sun, Wei Wang, Xiao Wang, Xiaobo Wang, et al. 2024. Mooncake: A KVCache-Centric Disaggregated Architecture for LLM Serving.
Xiao, Guangxuan, Yuandong Tian, Beidi Chen, Song Han, and Mike Lewis. 2024. “Efficient Streaming Language Models with Attention Sinks.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Zhang, Zhenyu, Sheng Shen, Derek Zhiyuan Cheng, Xingli Dong, and Kurt Keutzer. 2023. “H2O: Heavy-Hitter Oracle for Efficient Generative Inference of Large Language Models.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 36.

Agent workloads introduce the memory dilemma at the center of this chapter: execution state that idles during tool execution and subsequently resumes. When an agent invokes an external tool, its execution thread pauses while waiting for external API responses or environment feedback. During this idle window, the retained KV cache ties up expensive accelerator high-bandwidth memory. Work on tool interception formalized this challenge by framing KV cache lifecycle management around three primary operations. These were keeping the state resident in device memory, swapping it to host memory or storage, and dropping it entirely to recompute upon resumption (Abhyankar et al. 2024). This trade-off balances idle memory capacity against host-to-device transfer bandwidth and compute recomputation costs. Multi-turn serving systems such as CachedAttention and Mooncake expanded this design space into multi-tier memory hierarchies, paging dormant KV caches out to host DRAM and disaggregated solid-state storage clusters (Gao et al. 2024; Qin et al. 2024). Lossy approximation techniques pursued an alternative path, trading mathematical fidelity for reduced memory footprints through attention sinks (Xiao et al. 2024) and score-based eviction policies (Zhang et al. 2023). However, whether lossy cache approximation can reliably preserve the intricate, long-range dependencies required across extended agent trajectories remains an open systems and modeling challenge.

For readers seeking to trace the lineage from classical memory management to agent serving infrastructure, six works are especially illuminating:

  • Denning (1970) provides the foundational survey of paging, replacement algorithms, and locality of reference, showing how modern KV-cache management problems mirror classical virtual memory challenges transposed onto a new architectural storage unit.
  • Denning (1968a) introduces the working-set model, establishing the formal link between program locality and resource allocation. It provides the theoretical foundation for admission control, explaining why multi-tenant model servers must reject or queue requests when aggregate memory demand exceeds physical accelerator capacity.
  • Pope et al. (2023) develops an analytical model for large-model inference, demonstrating rigorously how attention head sharing, tensor partitioning, and memory bandwidth dictate whether a deployment is bounded by arithmetic throughput or memory capacity.
  • Kwon et al. (2023) introduces PagedAttention, adapting classical operating system paging, virtual block tables, and copy-on-write mechanisms to eliminate KV cache fragmentation in serving engines. It serves as an exemplary case study in translating classical systems principles to modern accelerator architectures.
  • Zheng et al. (2024) structures prompt cache management through radix trees, treating token prefix reuse as a first-class scheduling and eviction problem in multi-turn and agentic serving workloads.
  • Abhyankar et al. (2024) formalizes the tool-wait problem in agent workloads, quantifying the engineering trade-offs among retaining, swapping, and discarding KV cache state during external tool execution.
Denning, Peter J. 1970. “Virtual Memory.” ACM Computing Surveys 2 (3): 153–89. https://doi.org/10.1145/356580.356581.
Denning, Peter J. 1968a. “The Working Set Model for Program Behavior.” Communications of the ACM 11 (5): 323–33. https://doi.org/10.1145/363095.363141.
Kwon, Woosuk, Zhuohan Li, Siyuan Zhuang, et al. 2023. “Efficient Memory Management for Large Language Model Serving with Pagedattention.” Proceedings of the 29th Symposium on Operating Systems Principles, 611–26.
Zheng, Lianmin, Liangsheng Ying, Zhenghao Yin, Cody Hao Liu, Eric Yu, Prakhar Patel, Zhipeng Yang, Anthony K. Joseph, and Ion Stoica. 2024. “SGLang: Efficient Execution of Structured Language Model Programs.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2312.07104, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2312.07104.
Abhyankar, Reyna, Zijian He, Vikranth Srivatsa, Hao Zhang, and Yiying Zhang. 2024. “InferCept: Efficient Intercept Support for Augmented Large Language Model Inference.” Proceedings of the 41st International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 235: 81–95.

5.12 Exercises

Unless a problem states otherwise, use the hypothetical reference deployment in table 8. Its values are problem inputs chosen for round arithmetic, not measurements of any system. Take GB as \(10^9\) bytes, count \(2\Parameters\) weight operations per processed token, and model one engine iteration over \(\ForwardPassTokenCount\) token positions with the chapter’s weight-only estimate, \(\WeightOnlyStepTime(\ForwardPassTokenCount) = \max\left(5\;\text{ms},\; 2 \Parameters \ForwardPassTokenCount / \AttainableComputeRate\right)\).

1. Sizing retained state for a session target (Basic). An operator must hold 200 unshared sessions resident at once, each retaining 16,384 tokens.

  1. Compute \(\KvCacheBytesPerToken\) from equation 1, the payload \(\KvCacheMemory\) of one session, and the resident-fit bound in the pool.
  2. A multi-head variant of the same model stores 64 key-value heads per layer instead of 8. Compute its fit, and explain why no allocator policy can recover the difference.
  3. Two proposals would meet the target: storing KV at one byte per element, or having the harness cap retained context. Find the longest retained length that meets the target at two bytes per element. Then argue which proposal to try first and what evidence each must produce before it ships.

2. When paging pays (Basic). Static reservation sizes every request for a cap of 8,192 tokens. The request population’s mean final length is 3,072 tokens.

  1. What fraction of a reservation holds state at completion, and how many reservations fit in the pool?
  2. Paged allocation uses 32-token blocks, and final lengths have uniformly distributed remainders modulo the block size. Estimate the mean tail waste per sequence, its share of allocated KV memory, and the final-length snapshot count estimated by dividing the pool by the mean allocation. Repeat for 256-token blocks. Does this estimate alone determine live admission capacity?
  3. If the mean final length rose to 7,000 tokens under the same cap, how much of paging’s capacity advantage over static reservation would remain? Name one benefit of block allocation that does not depend on the length distribution.

3. Moving one field (Basic). A session sends four requests of 6,000 prompt tokens each. The first 4,500 tokens are invariant instructions and tool schemas, except for a request timestamp serialized after the first 300 tokens. The service reuses only complete 256-token blocks; the first request finds nothing cached, and each later request finds the earlier state resident, compatible, and permitted.

  1. Compute \(\PrefixHitRate\) from equation 2 over the four requests.
  2. Recompute it after the harness moves the timestamp to follow the invariant prefix.
  3. For layout (b), how much weight-only prefill does each hit avoid, and how much more would a service that also reuses partial tail blocks avoid? Which change should the team make first?

4. Choosing a chunk budget (Intermediate). Forty-eight requests are decoding when an 8,000-token uncached prompt arrives.

  1. If the engine runs the prompt as one unit, what gap between tokens do the ongoing decodes see?
  2. The ongoing streams must see at most 25 ms between tokens. Using equation 3 with one position per decode, find the largest budget \(\IterationTokenBudget\) and chunk size that meet the target, the number of iterations the prompt needs, and the time until its prefill completes.
  3. Ignoring the smaller final chunk, show that prefill completion is approximately \(\left(2 \Parameters \UncachedPromptLength/\AttainableComputeRate\right) \cdot \Time / \left(\Time - 2 \Parameters \lvert\DecodeRequestSet\rvert / \AttainableComputeRate\right)\) for an inter-token target \(\Time\) above the weight-read floor, where \(\UncachedPromptLength\) is the uncached prompt length. Find the tightest \(\Time\) that still completes the prompt within 500 ms.
  4. Explain why a target approaching \(2 \Parameters \lvert\DecodeRequestSet\rvert / \AttainableComputeRate\) is unworkable, and propose a design change for a service whose streams need 8 ms between tokens while new prompts need their prefill within 500 ms.

5. Reload, recompute, and a return burst (Intermediate).

  1. Evaluate equation 4 for the reference deployment. How much faster is reload than recomputation, and why does the answer not depend on retained length?
  2. Below what sustained link bandwidth would recomputation become the faster way back?
  3. Forty paused sessions of 32,768 tokens each return at the same instant. Compute each session’s state, its isolated reload time, the reload completion time of the last session when reloads share the full aggregate link rate, and the time to recompute all forty sequentially from weight work alone.
  4. Each returning session must be restored within 250 ms. How many of the forty meet that target if reloads share the link fairly, and how many if they are served one at a time in arrival order at the full link rate? Propose two design changes that would serve the rest.

6. Shared prefix and residence time (Intermediate). Requests carry 24,576 prompt tokens and an output allowance \(\OutputLimit = 1{,}024\). A block-aligned common prefix of 16,384 tokens is compatible and shareable across all of them. Blocks hold \(\KvBlockSize = 16\) tokens, and the planner withholds headroom \(\KvPoolHeadroomFraction = 0.10\). Each turn generates for 4 s and then waits 36 s for a tool.

  1. Using equation 6, compute the usable blocks and the resident-fit bound without and with sharing. Compare the gain with \(1/(1-\SharedPrefixFraction)\).
  2. The operator wants memory to sustain 10 turns/s. Using the shared fit and equation 7, what rate does keeping state through the wait sustain, and how many resident slots would the target require under that policy?
  3. Instead, each paused turn moves its private blocks to host memory and reloads them when the tool returns, while the common prefix stays resident for the other requests. Compute the memory-bound rate, the host-link traffic at 10 turns/s, and the restoration delay per turn. Is the link the binding resource?

7. Fan-out over a shared source bundle (Advanced). The research agent retrieves a 60,000-token bundle of source documents and fans the question out to 12 workers. Each worker’s input is the bundle followed by a 2,000-token private instruction, and each may generate up to 3,000 tokens. A synthesis call then reads the bundle followed by all 12 worker outputs. Ignore block rounding and headroom.

  1. A contiguous allocator deep-copies the bundle’s state into every worker. How much KV state does it copy? Compute the KV footprint of one question with and without block sharing, and how many questions each policy keeps resident in the pool.
  2. A developer serializes each worker’s private instruction before the bundle so that the model reads its task first. What happens to the shared footprint in (a), and what layout serves both goals?
  3. For the synthesis call, how much weight-only prefill does a hit on the bundle avoid, and how much new prefill remains? Contrast the constraint that binds this workload with the one that binds the repair agent in this chapter.
  4. A platform team proposes reusing the bundle’s KV state across different users’ questions that retrieve the same documents. State the conditions under which that reuse is compatible, available, and permitted, and explain why a hit says nothing about whether the documents are current or trustworthy.

8. Paused state through a human approval (Advanced). The remediation agent proposes a production change and waits for human approval. During a major incident, 60 trajectories await approval at once. Each retains 20,000 tokens: an 8,000-token invariant prefix, identical and shareable across trajectories, followed by 12,000 tokens of incident-specific diagnostics. Each turn generates for 6 s, approval takes 10 minutes, and the host can hold 1 TB of parked state.

  1. Under the keep policy, what share of the pool do the 60 trajectories hold without sharing and with the prefix shared, and what residency tax does the approval wait impose?
  2. Suppose the private blocks move to host memory and the shared prefix stays resident. Compute the host footprint, and compare the time to restore 20 trajectories whose approvals arrive together by reload with the time to recompute their private regions sequentially.
  3. Before acting on an approval, the harness must re-observe production state (principle 6), and the refreshed observations replace the diagnostics from token 8,000 onward. How much of the parked state remains usable? Recommend a paused-state policy, and say how the recommendation changes if refreshed observations were only appended after the existing diagnostics.
  4. Compared with the repair agent, what binds this workload, and how large is its restoration cost relative to the wait?

What’s Next: From reusable computation to durable information
Dropping the r18 request’s attention state forfeits no evidence, because the serving engine can rebuild it from the token log. The repair’s next session still needs that evidence once this serving session ends: the candidate at r18, the r17 failure, the passing reproducer, and the integration checks that remain unresolved. KV state can itself be moved or serialized, so storage location alone does not separate a cache from a memory. The unresolved problem is which information should persist, how it is identified and updated as its sources change, and how a later session retrieves evidence appropriate to its task. Long-Term Memory develops that durable-information contract.
